### F-EW-639 — Mother Geneviève's "spiritual bouquet"
- Claim: The foundress, Mother Geneviève of St Teresa, told her during a dark period: "serve God in peace and joy … our God is the God of peace." Thérèse took it as God-sent; the next Sunday Geneviève said she had had no revelation about her.
- When / where: 1889–1891, infirmary, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse (1805–1891)

- Notes: CF adds that two old Sisters were at the bedside and that Thérèse had been "severely tried, almost to sadness" that day.

- Claim: The invalid lay sister Sister Saint-Pierre, whom Thérèse helped to the refectory each evening at ten to six, herself asked that an account of it be kept, "to transmit to Carmel her admiration"; she also took care that it spread outside.

### F-EW-642 — People blamed the daughters' entry for Louis Martin's illness, even at recreation
- Claim: Opposition: During Louis Martin's illness (paralysis, then cerebral troubles; he had to be treated in a mental-health house), tactless people said in front of Thérèse that his daughters' entry into Carmel, and above all the youngest's, had caused it. Visitors said it at the parlour. In the community it was often discussed at recreation in front of the Martin sisters. Marie and Pauline were crushed; Thérèse stayed calm.
- When / where: 1889–1892, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Louis Martin; unnamed nuns and visitors
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW and HEARD

- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — first-hand

### F-EW-643 — Years of leading an old, crabby lay sister
- Claim: An old, infirm, sour-tempered lay sister (died 1895) was hard to guide between exercises because of her odd character and roughness. Thérèse offered herself for this task and did it for years, until she won the trust of the sister, who first received her very badly. She said she led her with the care she would give to leading Our Lord.

- People: Thérèse; "notre sœur X", an old lay sister
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW, and HEARD Thérèse

- Claim: The Divine Office was her "happiness and martyrdom" (F-EW-1536). On 12 Aug the Confiteor said before her Communion moved her to tears of "perfect contrition". On 19 Aug she nearly fainted when the Miserere was recited, even quietly, before Communion. In times of great family trouble she was happy to sing in choir "In te Domine speravi" (Ps 70). She quoted the Good Friday Office ("Responde mihi") to Pauline on 25 July.

- Claim: A picture of the Holy Face hung on the route from the choir to the refectory. ### F-EW-649 — Father's illness: harsh words in the parlour and "straitjackets" at recreation
- Claim: Louis Martin's paralysis grew worse shortly before Thérèse's clothing. He entered a special mental hospital on 12 February 1889. The sisters' grief was sharpened by careless talk. Once in the parlour they heard very harsh, contemptuous words about their father. At other times, at recreation, the mother prioress openly discussed his illness before them, the asylum's regime, what the mad do or can do, straitjackets and so on. Thérèse, only 16, never sought comfort from Marie or Pauline; they came to her to be comforted. She told them calmly this trial was one of the greatest graces of their life.
- When / where: 1889–1892, parlour and recreation
- People: Louis Martin; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Sr Agnès
- Witness: Mère Agnès, HEARD / present
- Source: PA 6, [469]–[470], S17 13 Jul 1915
- Original words: « la mère prieure … parlait … de ce que les fous font ou peuvent faire, des camisoles de force »
- Translation: "the mother prioress … spoke … of what madmen do or can do, of straitjackets"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: 1910 mentions "persons not delicate" blaming the daughters' entry. The prioress's recreation talk is new in 1915.

- Claim: Opposition: The superiors (ecclesiastical, unnamed) delayed her clothing and profession; stated reason: her youth. Thérèse took the habit on 10 Jan 1889, later than usual because of her age. ### F-EW-654 — Chastity: ashamed of her body as a child; the white lily; relics of virgins only
- Claim: As a child she said she was "ashamed of her body", consoled only that Jesus had taken one. In letters she exalted the "white lily" (virginity) against the "yellow lily", the sisters' code for marriage. At her clothing she chose only relics of virgins for her reliquary, even setting aside saints she loved more. She told Céline she never had temptations against chastity, and always acted alone as if someone were present.

### F-EW-655 — The dance: "You, the bride of Jesus?"
- Claim: When Céline was alone in the world with their father she had to follow social customs somewhat. Learning that Céline would attend an evening with dancing, Thérèse wept as she had never wept and called her to the parlour. Céline thought she was going too far — one could not make oneself ridiculous. Thérèse, indignant, said with force: "You, the bride of Jesus, want to compromise with the world?" Céline was stunned, obeyed, and kept her resolution at the cost of much trouble.
- When / where: c. 1889–93, Lisieux Carmel parlour
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline — took part
- Source: PO 4, f. 390r–v, S33
- Original words: « Toi, l'épouse de Jésus, tu veux donc pactiser avec le siècle »
- Translation: "You, the bride of Jesus, so you want to make terms with the world"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Céline had made a private vow of chastity (1889).
- Extraction ID: C-F196

### F-EW-668 — Other human details from MdA
- Claim: (a) At the Christmas plays Mère Agnès wrote, Thérèse played the Virgin holding the Child Jesus, recollected as if the child were alive; it brought tears. ### F-EW-711 — After her retreat, she avoided her own sisters — and was scolded by the foundress
- Claim: Opposition: After her long retreat (11 days apart from her sisters), Thérèse did not ask to visit them. At recreation her sisters expected her to seek them out; instead she sat by "the first one she met." Mère Agnès was deeply hurt. When the foundress Mère Geneviève heard, she scolded Thérèse very severely "to test her," saying she was acting like a heartless child and this was not true charity. Later the Jesuit Père Auriault, told the story by MdA, said: "Oh, I find that magnificent!"
- Opposition detail: Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse, foundress. Type: rebuke for avoiding her own sisters. Documented reason: to test her; such conduct was 'not true charity' and not the perfection religion asks.
- When / where: probably after the profession retreat, Sept 1890 (the source says "grande retraite"), Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès (Pauline); Marie; Céline (the three sisters); Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse; Père Auriault SJ
- Witness: MdA; she heard it from an older nun

Preparing the sacred vessels, she liked to see herself mirrored in the chalice and paten, thinking the host would rest where the gold reflected her.

- Claim: When the foundress Mère Geneviève died, families and workmen sent many wreaths. While Thérèse arranged them around the coffin, a watching sister said angrily that she put her own family's wreaths in front and the poor people's bouquets behind. Thérèse thanked her and asked for the moss cross sent by the workmen, to put it in front. The sister later admitted that from that day she regarded Thérèse as a saint.
- When / where: Dec 1891, choir of the Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; unnamed sister ("sœur…"); Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse (deceased)
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD the reply herself ("j'entendis")
- Source: PO 1, f. 216v–217r, Session 15, 29 Aug 1910
- New to English readers: partial (story is in HA ch. 12 but without Pauline as ear-witness)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The critic is not named.

- Claim: Opposition: An unnamed lay sister accused Thérèse of favouring her own family's flowers (charge: family pride, contempt for the poor). A lay sister said everyone could see these bouquets came from her family, since she put them forward and despised those from poor people. Marie waited for her reply; Thérèse looked at the sister kindly and at once put the less beautiful flowers in front.

### F-EW-749 — The influenza epidemic: Thérèse on her feet, Delatroëtte won over
- Claim: Opposition: Influenza killed three nuns. All but two or three sisters were in bed; the Office was suspended; there was "a silence of death." Thérèse moved among the sick and dying and in the sacristy with calm and presence of mind. The superior, Canon Delatroëtte, who had been so hostile to her entry, was struck each time he came and saw in her great hope for the community's future.

- Claim: Sr Fébronie caught influenza on 31 Dec 1891 after escorting Dr de Cornière to the enclosure door. In her agony she called in vain for her prioress (Gonzague, herself ill). She died 4 Jan 1892 at 8 p.m.; at the end only Thérèse and the infirmarian were with her.

### F-EW-755 — A preacher called her wish to be a saint "pride and presumption"
- Claim: She told a retreat preacher she wanted to be a saint and love God as much as St Teresa. He answered: what pride and presumption; correct your faults and moderate your rash desires. She quoted "Be perfect as your heavenly Father is perfect"; he was not convinced. She found approval only with Père Alexis (Prou), Recollect of Caen, at the 1892 retreat.

### F-EW-756 — She confronted Sr Marthe about flattering the prioress
- Claim: As a novice, her companion Sr Marthe de Jésus was misled by natural affection for Mère de Gonzague and got favours by flattery. Thérèse decided to enlighten her. She prepared by prayer for a meeting "very dangerous for her", since Sr Marthe could betray her to the prioress. She spoke with such authority and prudence that Sr Marthe was overcome and made good resolutions.
- When / where: c. 1892–1893 (Gonzague prioress)
- People: Thérèse; Sr Marthe de Jésus; Mère Marie de Gonzague

### F-EW-757 — The dance: "the golden statue of the world"
- Claim: When Céline was to attend a dance at a wedding, Thérèse was so alarmed she cried "as never before", and called Céline to the parlour. Céline thought she overdid it, since one could not "make oneself ridiculous". Thérèse was indignant and told her forcefully to consider the three young Hebrews who chose the furnace rather than bow to the golden statue; Céline, bride of Jesus (she had a vow of chastity), would bow to the world's golden statue.
- When / where: Carmel parlour, c. 1892 (wedding not named)
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline, involved
- Source: PA 8, [750]–[751], Session 35

- Translation: "Remember what I tell you on God's behalf!"

- Confidence: high
- Notes: See M-EW-021 for the sequel. A rare scene of Thérèse angry.
- Extraction ID: D-F251
### F-EW-758 — A disappointing community
- Claim: MM thought all Carmelites were saints, and was deeply disappointed. Little by little she saw many very imperfect nuns: silence, regularity and above all mutual charity were badly kept, and there were "lamentable divisions". The direction given to the community was largely to blame. In this setting Thérèse never committed the least fault.
- When / where: from 1892, Lisieux
- People: the community; (implicitly) Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: MM, observation
- Source: PO 18, f. 1104v, Session 67
- Original words: « il y avait entre les religieuses des divisions lamentables »
- Translation: there were lamentable divisions among the nuns
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — independent confirmation of MT
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: E-F147
### F-EW-759 — Distinctive: MM spied on her to catch a fault
- Claim: Distinctive: From the start MM saw Thérèse "did not resemble the other nuns", even the edifying ones. Because she heard many criticisms of Thérèse "inspired by party spirit", MM set out to catch her at fault. She watched her at the washing, the dishes, common work and recreation, and even tried to test her regularity. She never found a fault.
- When / where: 1892–1897
- People: MM; Thérèse
- Witness: MM, her own action
- Source: PO 18, f. 1104v–1105r

### F-EW-760 — MM avoided her: "I found her too perfect"
- Claim: MM never felt drawn to Thérèse; she even fled her. It was not lack of esteem: she found her too perfect, and a less perfect Thérèse would have encouraged her. MM never gave her any comfort. Yet Thérèse kept seeking her out with discretion, cheered her when she suffered, and often came to wash the dishes when it was MM's turn to dry, so as to talk alone with her. She showed confidence in MM to win hers.
- When / where: 1892–1897; kitchen / scullery
- People: MM; Thérèse
- Witness: MM, her own experience
- Source: PO 18, f. 1105v–1106r
- Original words: « Je la fuyais même. Ce n'était pas manque d'estime, au contraire, c'est que je la trouvais trop parfaite »
- Translation: I even fled from her. It was not lack of esteem; on the contrary, I found her too perfect
- New to English readers: partial (quoted by Piat)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: MJ confirms MM avoided her (F-EW-765). See E-Q14.
- Extraction ID: E-F152

- Claim: When the kitchen had a leftover no one wanted, it was given to Thérèse and she ate it without complaint. She told the lay sisters: never taste a dish needlessly. MM says she was not treated as sick and lacked the care her state required, yet never complained, and never showed she was cold.

### F-EW-780 — 8 Dec 1892: "your love for Mother Prioress is a poison"
- Claim: On 8 Dec 1892 Thérèse came before Mass and asked to talk. In her cell she looked sad, sat MJ beside her, embraced her ("which she never did") and showed great affection. Then she said God had made her feel she must speak: MJ's affection for Gonzague was too natural, a passion, "a poison" for religious; if so, she would have done better to stay in the world. She added that MJ could tell Gonzague everything; she preferred to be sent away from the monastery rather than fail in her duty. MJ wept, then admitted it was true.
- When / where: 8 Dec 1892 (Immaculate Conception), Thérèse's cell, before Mass
- People: Thérèse; MJ; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: MJ, experienced it
- Source: PO 15, f. 624v–626r (misprinted "695r"), Session 61; PA 18, p. 1073
- Original words: « je préfère être mal vue d'elle et qu'elle me renvoie du monastère si elle le veut, plutôt que de manquer à mon devoir »
- Translation: I would rather be in her bad books, and be sent away from the monastery if she wishes, than fail in my duty
- New to English readers: partial (HA Ms C 20–21 tells it without names)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: MJ says Thérèse "was not yet nineteen"; in fact she was 19 (born 2 Jan 1873). See E-S15, E-Q19.
- Extraction ID: E-F177
### F-EW-781 — 8 Dec 1892: Thérèse confronts Marthe about her attachment to Gonzague
- Claim: Marthe was attached to Gonzague "humanly". After praying about it, on 8 Dec 1892 Thérèse took Marthe aside, laid Marthe's head on her heart and held it there while she told her the whole truth: her affection was natural and put her soul in danger; she would have done better to stay in the world. Marthe wept, thanked her and asked her to keep correcting her. Thérèse added that if Gonzague noticed and asked, Marthe could tell her everything: she would rather be sent away from the monastery than fail in her duty.
- When / where: 8 Dec 1892
- People: Thérèse; Sr Marthe; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: Sr Marthe, participant

- New to English readers: partial — Ms C mentions such interventions without names

- Claim: Opposition: Céline says Thérèse was never properly novice mistress. ### F-EW-814 — Weekly permissions for life, because nobody told her
- Claim: Three years after profession novices leave the novitiate and ask general permissions monthly instead of weekly. After nine years she should have been freed of this, but no one told her, and she took care not to remind the prioress, who never thought of it. So all her life she asked her permissions every week.

### F-EW-815 — Poems composed at work, written only in evening free time
- Claim: She composed her poems while working during the day and waited for the free evening time to note them on paper. The sisters learned this only at the end. When Céline said she could easily have got permission to write them by day, she said she had carefully avoided permissions that would make religious life easy; if God did not let the prioress give it spontaneously, He wanted the sacrifice.

- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline, heard it

### F-EW-816 — The oratory mural of little angels
- Claim: Céline considers it "absolutely supernatural" that Thérèse, who had never learned painting, painted the mural in the oratory: a group of little angels each with an attribute. ### F-EW-817 — Thérèse stayed in the novitiate by her own request
- Claim: Thérèse had been professed four years in 1894 and should have left the novitiate the year before (nuns stayed three years after profession). Mère Marie de Gonzague, then novice mistress, told MT that Thérèse had asked to stay "out of humility". Agnès agreed because she hoped Thérèse would influence the novices. In 1896 Gonzague became prioress again but kept the novice-mistress title herself and left Thérèse as her auxiliary until her death.
- When / where: 1893–1897, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès

Thérèse, young and a novice, threatened her less, so Gonzague, though treating her severely, admired her maturity.

### F-EW-861 — Thérèse was never a capitular nun
- Claim: Although she should have left the novitiate three years after profession (Sept 1893), Thérèse stayed in it all her life. A common reading of the rules then allowed no more than two sisters of one family in the Chapter. Because Pauline and Marie were capitulars, Thérèse never had seat or vote in the conventual Chapter. She stayed with the novices as their "doyenne". Céline says Thérèse was glad to be counted "une petite".
- When / where: 1893–1897
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur
- Witness: editor's note in CS

- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high

### F-EW-910 — Illness chronology: throat 1894, cough 1896, creosote by the spoon
- Claim: When Céline entered in September 1894, Thérèse was already treated for a very inflamed throat. The illness became alarming only in April 1896. She hid the haemorrhage of Holy Thursday ("the accident of 4 April") and kept the full community life. During 1896 she developed a persistent cough. Treatments: tiring frictions, blistering plasters and cupping glasses, tincture of iodine, and creosote taken pure by the spoonful, not in capsules as later. Céline sees her still going at the set hour for her remedy, never forgetting it because it was unpleasant.
- When / where: 1894–97
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline, saw it
- Source: PA 8, [781]–[782], Session 37
- New to English readers: yes (treatments)
- Confidence: high; date inconsistency noted
- Notes: Holy Thursday 1896 was 2 April; "4 April" is an error.

### F-EW-911 — Mother Hermance's notes; chaplains, superior, gardener, doctor, turn sisters
- Claim: Mother Hermance du Cœur de Jésus esteemed her and during her illness passed Céline little notes for her and sent constant verbal messages. Céline heard that the chaplain Abbé Youf valued her and trusted her; the superior, Canon Delatroëtte, seemed to esteem her greatly. The sacristan-gardener said that though she was veiled he recognised her by her grave, religious bearing. Abbé Faucon, replacing the sick chaplain, heard her confession shortly before her death and came out very moved, saying she was "confirmed in grace". Dr de Cornière was edified by her patience and heavenly smile, as were the turn sisters who came in on Sundays to watch her during Mass.
- When / where: Carmel, 1894–97
- People: Mother Hermance du Cœur de Jésus; Abbé Louis Youf; Canon Delatroëtte; the sacristan-gardener (unnamed); Abbé Faucon; Dr de Cornière; the turn sisters

### F-EW-918 — She often wept when speaking of Jesus's love
- Claim: MT often saw Thérèse shed tears while speaking to her about Jesus's love and her own wish to love him. In PA: she exhorted with such unction that "she often wept".
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, saw it
- Source: PO 17, f. 1072r; PA 21, p. 1238
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: E-F23
### F-EW-919 — The trying first portress
- Claim: For two or three years Thérèse was second portress under an elderly first portress. MT describes her as a very good nun but painfully slow, full of manias, able "to make an angel lose patience". In PA MT adds she talked all day in sermons that were "real charades". When MT answered her impatiently, the old sister said Thérèse had never spoken to her like that. Thérèse told MT: be gentle, "she is ill", and imagine having to listen to her all day as I do.
- When / where: c. 1894–1896, the turn / portress duty
- People: Thérèse; unnamed elderly first portress; MT
- Witness: MT, experienced part and heard Thérèse's reply
- Source: PO 17, f. 1075v–1076r; PA 21, p. 1246–1247
- Original words: « jamais sœur Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus ne m'a parlé comme vous le faites! »
- Translation: Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus has never spoken to me the way you do!
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: MT says (PA) this patience alone would earn canonization. The sister is not named; merger may identify her from other sources.
- Extraction ID: E-F31
### F-EW-920 — She sat beside sad sisters at common work
- Claim: MT often saw Thérèse choose a place next to sisters who looked sad during common work. She could not speak, so she smiled at them and looked for ways to help. When she could not speak to a sister in trouble she prayed Jesus to console her.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MT, saw it
- Source: PO 17, f. 1075r–v; PA 21, p. 1246
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: E-F33
### F-EW-921 — "It is not our Mother you love, it is yourself"
- Claim: MT complained that Mère Agnès preferred talking with another sister. Expecting sympathy, she got a lesson: if she truly loved the prioress she would be glad to see her find pleasure with others. MT says she understood for the first time what unselfish love was.
- When / where: 1894–1896 (Agnès prioress)
- People: Thérèse; MT; Mère Agnès
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1076v–1077r; PA 21, p. 1240–1241
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Shows MT's attachment to Agnès.
- Extraction ID: E-F37
### F-EW-922 — Thérèse as peacemaker in stormy quarrels
- Claim: MT says there were disputes in the community, "sometimes very stormy", because of Gonzague's mentality and "certain spirits". When things became too poisoned, it was always Thérèse who, with unusual tact, restored peace.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: MT, general observation

### F-EW-923 — Tears collected in a shell
- Claim: MT cried often "over nothing". Thérèse fought this and, to cure her, made her collect her tears in a shell each time she wanted to cry. MT says this "original means" worked perfectly.
- When / where: undated, 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, experienced it
- Source: PA 21, p. 1249, Session 63, 26 Sept 1916; PO 17, f. 1080r (weeping only); editors' note PO 17 introduction
- Original words: « me faire recueillir chaque fois mes larmes dans une coquille »
- Translation: to make me collect my tears in a shell each time

- Notes: The editors confirm MT is the unnamed novice of this story.

### F-EW-924 — No curious questions in direction
- Claim: MT admired that Thérèse never asked awkward or curious questions, even "for her good". She listened to confidences with interest but did not draw them out.

### F-EW-925 — Choir posture: no movement, not touching face or clothes
- Claim: Thérèse insisted on good posture in choir. She herself moved as little as possible and never touched her face or clothes. She said God is pleased to see we pay attention to him. In PA: if you had an audience with an earthly king, every movement would be studied. She also told MT she sometimes prayed more easily during the Divine Office than during mental prayer.
- When / where: 1894–1897, choir
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, saw and heard
- Source: PO 17, f. 1081r; PA 21, p. 1230

### F-EW-926 — Way of the Cross for the souls in purgatory
- Claim: Thérèse loved the Way of the Cross and said she would make it daily if she could. She did it for her own good and for the souls in purgatory. Work set by obedience sometimes prevented it. She also tried to gain every indulgence for the dead.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1081v; PA 21, p. 1227, 1248

### F-EW-927 — Direction before the statue of the smiling Virgin
- Claim: When MT had hard things to say in direction, Thérèse took her before the miraculous statue that had smiled on her in childhood. She said: tell it to the Blessed Virgin, not to me. She listened beside MT, then had her kiss Mary's hand and gave her advice.
- When / where: 1894–1897, before the statue of the Virgin of the Smile (in the monastery)
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, experienced it
- Source: PO 17, f. 1081v; PA 21, p. 1231

### F-EW-928 — No frowning: the face mirrors the soul
- Claim: Thérèse could not bear the least contraction on MT's face, like wrinkling the forehead. She said the face should be calm like a contented child's, even when alone, because we are always seen by God and the angels. She had a devotion to her guardian angel and kept dignified posture out of respect for him.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1082r; PA 21, p. 1231–1232

### F-EW-930 — Gonzague's jealous scenes against Mère Agnès
- Claim: Opposition: While Mère Agnès was prioress (1893–1896), Gonzague's "touchy and jealous" character showed in wounding behaviour toward her. In PA MT calls them "scandalous scenes of jealousy" made daily. Thérèse stayed calm though she felt it for her "little mother". She said Agnès would have the martyrs' crown: "she is a saint, that is why God does not spare her".
- When / where: 1894–1896
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès; Thérèse

### F-EW-931 — Neighbour at table: no visible likes or dislikes
- Claim: MT sat next to Thérèse in the refectory and watched closely, but could never tell what she liked or disliked. Only shortly before her death, pressed by the infirmarian, did Thérèse admit certain foods had always made her ill. MT had seen her eat them each time with indifference.
- When / where: 1894–1897, refectory
- People: Thérèse; MT; the infirmarian
- Witness: MT, saw it

### F-EW-932 — Small refectory mortifications
- Claim: Thérèse told MT not to mix foods to improve their taste, not to lean her back against the wall (the narrow benches touch the wall), and to end each meal with something tasteless such as a bite of dry bread. In her cell MT should keep her stool away from the wall. Thérèse said these "little nothings" do not harm health and are not noticed.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1085r; PA 21, p. 1255–1256
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: MT insists Thérèse gave no advice she did not practise.
- Extraction ID: E-F64
### F-EW-933 — Cold was her worst bodily mortification
- Claim: Thérèse told MT the cold was the hardest bodily penance in Carmel: she suffered it so much she thought one could not suffer more. She never allowed herself relief. When MT warmed her sandals (alpargates) on a foot-warmer and put them on hot, Thérèse said she would have thought that a great lack of mortification. One must not even show one is cold: no stooping, no shivering.
- When / where: winters 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1085v; PA 21, p. 1256
- Original words: « J'en ai souffert tellement que je crois qu'il n'est pas possible d'en souffrir davantage »
- Translation: I suffered from it so much that I believe it is not possible to suffer more
- New to English readers: partial (Thérèse's words on the cold are in the Last Conversations)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See MJ's chilblains, F-EW-2273.
- Extraction ID: E-F65
### F-EW-934 — Poverty: close-lined paper, low wick, saved salt
- Claim: Thérèse chose paper with close lines and wrote on every line to save paper. She turned her small lamp's wick very low. In the refectory, if she took a few grains of salt too many, she kept them under her napkin for another meal. She mended her clothes to the end. She said nothing is sweeter than lacking necessities, and one should always buy the cheapest.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MT, saw it
- Source: PO 17, f. 1087r; PA 21, p. 1264–1265
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: E-F70
### F-EW-935 — Do not write your vocation story
- Claim: Shortly before Thérèse began writing her life, MT said she would write the story of her vocation to reread later. Thérèse told her not to, not to ask permission, and said she herself would write nothing about her life without an unsolicited order. It is humbler not to write about oneself.
- When / where: c. late 1894 / Jan 1895
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1089v–1090r
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: MT did not know Thérèse was writing Ms A (f. 1067v). Useful for dating and for Thérèse's motive in writing.
- Extraction ID: E-F81
### F-EW-936 — MT's homesickness for the Paris Carmel
- Claim: MT found it hard to adapt to Lisieux customs after over two years in Paris. Constant comparison made her unhappy. Thérèse saw that these thoughts would wreck her vocation and fought them. When MT dwelt on them, Thérèse corrected her without being told; when she resisted, Thérèse showed pleasure.
- When / where: 1894–1895
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1092r, Session 66, 15 Mar 1911
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See F-EW-945 (her "secret").

### F-EW-937 — Little sympathy for a nun "from another Carmel"
- Claim: MT says her lack of virtue and health, and the little sympathy she met in the community because she came from another Carmel, made "a thousand almost insurmountable difficulties". Thérèse alone comforted her and skilfully pleaded her cause with the sisters who were against her. Thérèse said she would gladly give her life for MT's vocation to succeed.
- When / where: 1894–1896
- People: MT; Thérèse; unnamed opposing sisters
- Witness: MT
- Source: PA 21, p. 1247, Session 63
- Original words: « Que de bon cœur je donnerais ma vie pour que vous réussissiez dans votre vocation! »

- New to English readers: yes

### F-EW-938 — "Do you trust you will succeed anyway?"
- Claim: In MT's first novitiate year, when all seemed hopeless for her vocation, Thérèse asked if she still trusted she would succeed. MT said yes. Thérèse said keep it: God measures his gifts by our trust. She then admitted that if MT had wavered, she herself would have doubted, "so lost is all hope humanly".
- When / where: 1894–1895
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT

### F-EW-939 — She tried to open Gonzague's eyes
- Claim: Thérèse suffered to see Gonzague blind to her faults and tried "all means" to make her see the truth. She often exposed herself to Gonzague's ill-will this way, but she loved "this unhappy soul" in spite of all.

- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague

### F-EW-940 — Painting gifts for the prioress's feast: complaints, not thanks
- Claim: Opposition: Before the prioress's feast, almost every sister brought her gift to Thérèse to be decorated with painting. Each wanted to be served first. Instead of thanks Thérèse often got reproaches: "you took more care over sister so-and-so's work, you started with her". Some demanded very complicated paintings. She overworked to please them and rarely succeeded, but it did not disturb her peace.
- When / where: before feasts of the prioress, 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; many unnamed sisters
- Witness: MT, observation

### F-EW-941 — Sisters took advantage of her kindness
- Claim: Thérèse always helped at once when disturbed, even for no reason, with a smile. MT saw many sisters abuse this and ask her help as a thing owed. MT was sometimes "revolted" by it; Thérèse found it natural.
- When / where: 1894–1897

- Claim: Thérèse often begged MT to report every unpleasant word she heard, not only about her but, more painful, about her "Little Mother" (Agnès) and her other sisters. MT saw that she received these reports with supernatural joy.

- Claim: Distinctive: MT names as a mark of heroism that, with three sisters and a cousin in the house and her favourite sister as prioress, Thérèse never, to MT's knowledge, took the easy comforts this allowed. She even seemed to prefer the company of other nuns. MT stresses this was not coldness: she loved her sisters, especially Agnès, with great tenderness.

### F-EW-944 — Distinctive: her ordinary life was the point
- Claim: Distinctive: MT says Thérèse had none of the miracles, ecstasies and gifts usually admired in saints. Her life "did not leave the ordinary", which makes her imitable by all. God made her feel he wanted it so, to make her a model for souls walking in the dark night of faith.

- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MT, her judgement and Thérèse's remarks
- Source: PA 21, p. 1273–1274, Session 64

- Translation: her life here below never left the ordinary; that is her special mark, which makes her imitable and within reach of all
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The editors praise these pages as among the best of the Process.
- Extraction ID: E-F107
### F-EW-945 — Her "secret" for reading souls: invoke the Virgin first
- Claim: MT often felt Thérèse read her soul, correcting thoughts MT had not told her. Asked how, Thérèse said: my secret is that I never correct you without first asking the Blessed Virgin what will do you most good. She was often surprised herself at what she said without reflecting. In PA she first denies the gift: "I do not have that gift at all."
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1092r; PA 21, p. 1274
- Original words: « Je n'ai pas du tout ce don, mais voici mon secret »
- Translation: I do not have that gift at all, but here is my secret

### F-EW-946 — MT tried to find a fault in her and failed
- Claim: Because Thérèse corrected her faults, MT wanted to find some imperfection in her to excuse herself, but never did. MT says what is said of Thérèse now, even by her own sisters, is still below what she saw.

### F-EW-947 — Poems and plays were all MT knew of her writings
- Claim: MT did not know during Thérèse's life that she was writing her story by obedience. She knew only the poems and short plays Thérèse composed for community feasts. After her death, reading HA, she found it matched what Thérèse had told her.

### F-EW-948 — MT's sulk broken by "a superior force"
- Claim: MT was annoyed that Thérèse would not see her when she came at a bad time, and sulked, not speaking to her all day. In the evening Thérèse came to her. As MT got ready to reproach her, she was suddenly seized by a feeling that made her apologize, and she understood she was dealing with "a saint particularly loved by God". From then on she felt a growing respect for her.
- When / where: undated, 1894–1897
- People: MT; Thérèse
- Witness: MT, her own experience

### F-EW-949 — Opposition: Aimée fought Céline's entry (editors' note)
- Claim: Opposition: The editors say Sr Aimée was never intimate with Thérèse. She did not favour four sisters living in the same monastery and opposed Céline's entry "with all her strength", then changed her mind "mysteriously". Thérèse took this change as God's answer that her father had gone straight to heaven (Ms A 82v). At the PO Aimée called herself "one of the instruments God used to sanctify her"; she dropped this at the PA.

### F-EW-950 — She carried the heavy statue of the Virgin of the Smile alone
- Claim: When the statue that had smiled at Thérèse in her childhood illness came to the monastery, no sister could carry it; all found it too heavy. Thérèse said it was not too heavy for her, seized it and carried it easily to the oratory meant for it.
- When / where: undated (after the statue came to the Carmel, probably 1894), Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; the community
- Witness: TSA, apparently SAW

### F-EW-951 — Making banners for the Compiègne martyrs' centenary
- Claim: For the centenary of the Carmelite martyrs of Compiègne (17 July 1894), the Compiègne Carmel asked Lisieux to help decorate its chapel. TSA and Thérèse were given the job of making banners (oriflammes) together. Thérèse was overjoyed and said: what happiness if we had the same fate!

- People: Thérèse; TSA
- Witness: TSA, SAW and HEARD

### F-EW-952 — Family quarrels in the parlour calmed by the youngest
- Claim: MdA often accompanied the three Martin sisters to the parlour when their uncle M. Guérin came. If disagreements arose over family affairs, "these little clouds" quickly vanished under Thérèse's influence. Her sisters asked the youngest for advice, and what she said was "gospel."

### F-EW-953 — A lay-sister novice accused Thérèse and Marthe of plotting to have her sent away
- Claim: A white-veil novice accused Thérèse and Marthe of taking steps to have her dismissed from the monastery. It was entirely false. Marthe was furious; Thérèse told her they must redouble their tenderness to this sister, smile at her and serve her in small things.

### F-EW-954 — Asked for the smallest parts, got the best: "my hoarse voice"
- Claim: In the novitiate's little feasts, Thérèse asked for the most hidden part, but was given the finest ones, which she did very well, claiming they suited her hoarse voice. (She had a sore throat.)

- Claim: From 1894–1895 Sr Marie de St Joseph had Mère Agnès's permission to ask Thérèse for advice, treating her as a spiritual authority. After Thérèse's death she had to leave the Carmel for treatment but kept writing to Mère Agnès; in 1929 she wrote that Thérèse continued the work of sanctifying her.

### F-EW-956 — She did not confide her own pains to novices
- Claim: Céline noticed Thérèse held back from speaking about herself. Her principle: a superior must forget herself, and when someone tells her of a pain or health trouble, she must not complain of the same.
- When / where: 1894–97
- Witness: Céline — SAW

- Claim: Céline copied from a life of the Desert Fathers (Fr Michel-Ange Marin) the story of the sinner Paësie, saved by one hour of repentance. Thérèse was so struck that she regretted not putting it in her autobiography and urgently asked that it be added.

### F-EW-986 — She ran off after welcoming Céline into Carmel
- Claim: When Céline entered on 14 September 1894, Thérèse embraced her like all the others and was already hurrying off when the prioress signed to her to take Céline to her cell; she would not have come without that call. At the clothing of their cousin Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie she did not go to the enclosure door to hand her back to the family; when Céline reproached her, she said she stayed away because she wanted it too much.

- Claim: When Céline entered (14 Sept 1894), the statue of the Virgin of the Smile was brought in. Thérèse went to the enclosure door, seized it in a quick movement, held it with love, and carried it off as easily as a feather, though it was very heavy (solid plaster, 0.90 m). The sisters present were surprised. In her last illness it was placed facing her bed and she looked at it constantly.

### F-EW-2595 — Isidore Guérin's newspaper campaign: Le Normand and Henry Chéron (Oct–Nov 1891)
- Claim: On 23 Oct 1891 the young lawyer Henry Chéron (24), founder of the anticlerical Le Progrès lexovien, attacked Leo XIII in an article on a letter to Mgr Gouthe-Soulard, saying the Pope was 'derailing'. Isidore knew him: in 1884 Chéron had served him for some months as a pharmacy student and had taught Marie the accordion and been heard by little Thérèse; he then played hymns and had brought a colleague back to Easter duty. On 3 Nov 1891 Isidore answered in Le Normand, a paper about to fail for lack of money and writers, signing as 'A Vaticanard who asks that his faith be respected'. His article repeated 'He derails, the one who...' six times in praise of Leo XIII (including his encyclical on workers). He then rescued the paper financially, became its chief editor for six years, refused a duel, and wrote (Piat counts) 74 signed pieces in 1893 alone. He handed the editorial to a successor in early 1896 because of illness, and kept giving advice and money.
- When / where: 23 Oct – 3 Nov 1891; 1892–1896, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin; Henry Chéron; Leo XIII; Thérèse (in a remark)
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La victoire sur le monde' and footnote 1; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: Thérèse, proud, said (Piat quotes) that her uncle's arm 'never ceases to tire in writing admirable pages that must save souls and make demons tremble'. A political enemy of the Guérins who later helped Thérèse's cult (see next card).
- Extraction ID: N-F146

### F-EW-2599 — Isidore Guérin cares for the paralysed Louis Martin (1889–1894)
- Claim: While Louis Martin was in the Bon Sauveur hospital at Caen, Isidore took Léonie and Céline into his home and treated them as his daughters. On 10 May 1892 he brought his brother-in-law back to Lisieux, settled at 7 rue Labbey near his own house. In the summers of 1893 and 1894 he took him to La Musse despite the difficulty of taking, by rail to La Bonneville and then by road, the old man, his carriage and his paralytic's bed. Louis thanked him: 'In heaven I will repay you.' Isidore, Piat says, saw in this humiliating old age 'the august crown of a life of immolation'.
- When / where: 1889–1894, Lisieux and La Musse
- People: Isidore Guérin; Louis Martin; Léonie; Céline

### F-EW-2604 — Thérèse's letters to Marie Guérin, 1889–1890: LT 92, LT 93, LT 109
- Claim: Piat says the correspondence began in banalities and ended in an intense dialogue in which Thérèse's mastery of soul guidance became evident. On 24 Apr 1889 (Louis Martin's illness) Thérèse wrote that after the terrible trial they were 'more sisters than before'. In May 1889 the Guérins, with Léonie and Céline, visited the Paris Exposition (the Revolution's centenary); Marie, lost in the crowd, saw evil everywhere and dared not receive Communion, writing to Thérèse that Paris was 'a real hell'. Thérèse's reply of 30 May 1889 (LT 92): 'You have not committed the shadow of a sin. You must despise all these temptations... When the devil has succeeded in keeping a soul from Communion he has won everything... and Jesus weeps.' Piat says Pius X later expressed wonder at its eucharistic doctrine and this hastened the introduction of the cause. LT 93 (14 Jul 1889): thorns can save a soul. Marie's reply of 23 Jul 1889 shows she had taken the religious name 'Marie du Saint-Sacrement' and made a monastic 'cell' at La Musse. LT 109 (27–29 Jul 1890): 'I know no other means to perfection but love.'
- When / where: 24 Apr 1889 – 29 Jul 1890
- People: Thérèse; Marie Guérin; Pius X
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La victoire sur le monde'; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high on the letter numbers and dates (LT 92, 93, 109 as given by Piat's editors)
- Notes: Piat's statement that Pius X's astonishment at LT 92 hastened the cause is his claim; no date is given. The circular cards F-EW-2570 and F-EW-2571 quote the same letters. Compare Thérèse's own words on scruples in Ms A.
- Extraction ID: N-F155
### F-EW-2605 — 24 Sept 1890: at Thérèse's veiling, Marie's doubts vanish; the 'heartless' joke at the grille
- Claim: Piat says that at Thérèse's Prise de voile on 24 Sep 1890, while Thérèse lay on the floor for the great prostration, Marie Guérin felt her last doubts vanish: she would be a Carmelite. Céline was the first to hear the confession; on Thérèse's insistence and Père Pichon's advice Marie had chosen Céline as confidante ('I have shown her my whole soul'). One day when Marie complained of missing Céline, who was on pilgrimage to Paray-le-Monial, Thérèse gently reproached her for lack of detachment. Marie protested and in the heat of the talk called her 'heartless'. Thérèse, who 'mimed to perfection', put on her most offended look and shut the grille in her face, then reopened it a moment later, laughing aloud at her visitor's stunned face.
- When / where: 24 Sep 1890; c. 1892–1894, Lisieux Carmel parlour
- People: Thérèse; Marie Guérin; Céline; Père Pichon
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La victoire sur le monde' (end); rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: A scene: Thérèse's humour and skill at mimicry at the grille. Céline's Paray-le-Monial pilgrimage date is not given here.

### F-EW-2606 — Louis Martin's death at La Musse, 29 July 1894, and Marie's letters of 1895
- Claim: Piat says Louis Martin died at La Musse on 29 Jul 1894, surrounded by delicate attention. He was still sensitive to the landscape: the nightingale's trills in the woods, the coaxing voice of his goddaughter, or her fingers playing his favourite piece at the piano, 'Rêverie' by Rosellen. On 10 Aug 1893 Thérèse thanked Mme Guérin: God had done for Louis what he did for Job. Céline entered Carmel on 14 Sep 1894 (Exaltation of the Cross) with Marie in tears at the door. On 17 May 1895 Marie wrote to Céline from La Musse about visiting the room where Louis died, and the words inscribed on the walls 'Judge not and you shall not be judged'; she recalled Céline's grief and 'the scene of the last kiss', and hearing at night the noise of the small carriage that took Louis out. On the anniversary she wrote that she was struck by 'a serious, calm feeling that speaks of the other world', and could feel only happiness at his release.
- When / where: 29 Jul 1894; 10 Aug 1893; 14 Sep 1894; May–July 1895, La Musse / Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; Marie Guérin; Céline; Thérèse; Mme Guérin
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness

### F-EW-2646 — Marie's 73 letters to her cousin Céline Maudelonde (1894–1904) and their advice
- Claim: Piat says seventy-three letters from Marie to Céline Maudelonde survive, from 1894 to 24 Nov 1904. Céline, who had married and become a mother, sent Marie a kind of inner journal; the replies give advice on sanctifying duties, on using illness and trial, and on books to read. Marie's advice echoes Thérèse: 'Do everything out of love'; 'Say a Thank-you without taste, without fervour, but say it all the same... a spiritual communion'; 'A pin-prick is often more painful than a lance thrust'; 'Above all do not let the devil tighten your heart; when he has put sadness in a soul he has won everything'; 'It is a Father, you know... he does not put the knife to our throat. Good will is enough'; 'On each bead of the rosary say to Jesus that I love him'. She also compared her cousin's two small children to 'true little masters' of the way of abandonment.
- When / where: 1894–24 Nov 1904, Lisieux Carmel (letters to Céline Maudelonde)
- People: Marie Guérin; Céline Maudelonde; Thérèse

### F-EW-2662 — The Guérin family: what became of Dr La Néele and Jeanne; the family tomb
- Claim: Piat says Dr La Néele survived his father-in-law by a few years and died without children on the feast of St Joseph, 19 Mar 1916. Jeanne La Néele alone lived to see Thérèse's triumphs; she died on 25 Apr 1938 under the roof of her adopted daughter at Nogent-le-Rotrou. In the Lisieux cemetery the Guérin family monument bears the inscription 'O Crux, Ave, Spes unica'; Isidore (Brother Elie of the Blessed Sacrament) lies next to the Carmelite graves, where Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie, exhumed in 1923, rests in the vault once occupied by Thérèse. A little lower, a granite cross marks the grave where in 1894 Isidore had gathered, beside Louis Martin's coffin, that of his wife and their four children who died young.

### F-EW-2665 — Sr Saint-Stanislas and Thérèse in the sacristy (1891–1893): 'slow and little devoted', 'little sister Amen'
- Claim: The biography of Sr Saint-Stanislas says mutual esteem and affection marked her relations with Thérèse, despite contrasts between the tiny old nun of 64 and the tall postulant of 15. Sr Saint-Stanislas sighed a little for 'little Sister Amen' (Thérèse) who spent 'a crazy amount of time' scraping candles and the censer. ## Part 6 — School, illness and First Communion (1881–1886)
### F-LF-270 — Clothing, 10 January 1889: white velvet, Alençon lace and snow
- Claim: Her reception of the habit came on 10 Jan 1889, later than the usual six months after entry; Laveille's footnote attributes the delay to Delatroëtte's continued opposition on grounds of her youth. Before it she made a retreat "in aridity" and wrote to Mère Agnès: "In my soul's intercourse with Jesus there is nothing, nothing but dryness and sleep! Since my Well-beloved wills to remain asleep, I will not prevent Him." At Louis's wish she wore a dress of white velvet edged with swansdown and trimmed with point d'Alençon lace, her long fair hair in natural curls, and white lilies as her only ornament. She wrote that Louis waited at the enclosure door, moved to tears, "pressed me to his heart, saying: 'Ah, here is my little Queen'", gave her his arm and they entered the chapel together: "This day was his triumph, his last feast on earth" (HA ch. vii p.124). Céline told Louis she too would enter Carmel; he said, "Let us go together before the Blessed Sacrament to thank the Saviour… Yes, the good God greatly honours me in claiming my children. If I possessed anything better, I would hasten to offer it to Him." Thérèse wrote that snow covered the quadrangle when she entered the cloister, though the weather had been mild; the community spoke of "the little miracle". On 2 Jan 1889 she wrote to Mme Guérin thanking her for the wreath of lilies sent by her cousins for the clothing.
- When / where: 10 Jan 1889; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Louis; Céline; Mère Agnès; Delatroëtte; Mme Guérin
- Source: LAV p.212–215, p.451 (letter of 2 Jan 1889, Appendix)
- Source type: own-writing / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: "habit 10 Jan 1889")
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Laveille says the weather was "extremely mild" that day; no weather record was checked. Thérèse's snow remark: see M-LF-005. Surprise: the dress used Alençon lace, the product of her mother's trade. It is the last feast Louis attended before the illness (third attack "a month after her reception", LAV p.216).
### F-LF-198 — 12 February 1889: the revolver and the committal to the Bon-Sauveur
- Claim: On Tuesday 12 Feb 1889 Louis was taken to the Bon-Sauveur asylum at Caen, at his family's request, so they could take him out at will. Céline wrote to Pauline Romet on 18 Feb 1889: "the greatest of trials"; "in his imagination he saw terrible things, carnage, battles; he heard the cannon and the drum"; an attempted theft in the town confirmed his fears, and he wanted to carry his revolver "in case of danger", saying he "would not wish to harm even a cat". She wrote that with the revolver he was far from wishing to harm them: "he wished on the contrary to defend us". She added that "perhaps we should have waited and tried, before acting, every means to take it from him". (The blog by O'Riordan, citing the Carmel archive, says the revolver alarmed Isidore Guérin, who then had him taken to Caen; Céline's letter does not name Isidore.) The asylum doctor said "paralysie générale, tout le système nerveux est attaqué". Céline added that in town he was called "le saint Patriarche" and that she wished to live as a "dame en chambre" in a community near him. The admission certificate was signed by Dr Notta. Louis was patient no. 14,449, in the "Saint-Joseph" section with about 500 men judged calm or semi-calm. The asylum held about 1,700 patients. The entry in the register reads "Manifestations délirantes avec détention d'arme" (per handoff notes). Thérèse's list of graces calls 12 Feb 1889 "notre grande Richesse" (Ms A 86r).
- When / where: 12 Feb 1889; Lisieux; Caen
- People: Louis; Isidore Guérin; Céline; Léonie; Dr Notta
- Source: Céline to "ma chère Tante" Pauline Romet, Lisieux, 18 Feb 1889, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/de-celine-a-pauline-romet-18-fevrier-1889/ (raw-notes/letters-text/de-celine-a-pauline-romet-18-fevrier-1889.txt); Carmel page "Le couple Martin" (raw-notes/carmel-pages/le-couple-martin.txt); O'Riordan blog https://www.louisandzeliemartin.org/blessed-louis-and-zelie-martin-blog/tag/Bon+Sauveur (facts only)
- Source type: eyewitness / scholarship
- Rights: unknown / facts-only
- Original words: « dans son imagination il voyait des choses épouvantables, des carnages, des batailles ; il entendait le canon et le tambour »
- Translation: in his imagination he saw terrible things, carnage, battles; he heard the cannon and the drum.
- New to English readers: yes (baseline gives no details)
- Confidence: high for Céline's letter; medium for numbers (blog).
- Notes: Laveille says only "a Home chosen by his family" (LAV p.216) and never names Caen. The Carmel names the illness "artério-sclérose cérébrale"; a blogger suggests possibly Alzheimer's (opinion). Interdiction: F-LF-199. Céline's own words show doubt about the committal.
### F-LF-199 — Louis's interdiction, 8 June 1889, "the sharpest wound"
- Claim: The Carmel essay says Louis had to sign the renunciation of the management of his property on 8 Jun 1889; the trial was heavy and the humiliation deep. Céline wrote in 1909 that through "an unfortunate misunderstanding" lawyers came to the Bon-Sauveur to have him sign the papers of his legal "interdiction", telling him it was at his children's request. He wept, said his children were abandoning him, and signed. She calls this the sharpest wound.

### F-LF-200 — Céline and Léonie in Caen; the moves to the rue Paul-Banaston
- Claim: Céline and Léonie lodged with the Sisters of Saint Vincent de Paul in Caen until 14 May 1889 to visit Louis, sometimes getting only news from a nun. Then they went back to Lisieux. The Guérins lived from 25 Mar to 7 Jun 1889 at "La Maison Sauvage", 16 rue Condorcet, then at Les Buissonnets with the two girls; in July they were at La Musse. In autumn 1889 all moved to 19 rue Paul-Banaston (formerly rue de la Chaussée), a house Isidore bought on 20 Apr 1889 from Dr Notta. Céline and Léonie lived there until Louis's discharge. The Buissonnets lease ended at Christmas 1889 and Les Buissonnets was returned to the owner. After that the sisters visited Caen every week "without fail" (Céline).

### F-LF-201 — How Louis, the town and the family saw the trial
- Claim: Céline wrote in 1909 that at her first visit Louis had good days and understood his situation. When the asylum doctors said they would cure him, he said he did not wish it: the trial was a mercy, to expiate his pride. The nursing Sister called him a saint. In the town he was called "le saint Patriarche" (Céline, 18 Feb 1889). Céline says "friends" spoke like Job's friends, and voices spoke of a "broken future". In the worst of the trial the family placed a marble ex-voto under the Holy Face in the Carmel chapel: "Blessed be the name of the Lord". On 1 Mar 1889 Céline wrote that "l'opinion du monde ne pèse en rien" and Sr Costard said Louis was paralysed, his tongue a little thick, moving slowly. Céline wrote that she and the family "hid from him" that they thought him ill before the crisis.

### F-LF-202 — Thérèse's words on her father's trial
- Claim: Laveille quotes letters to Céline: Jan 1889, "We have now nothing further to hope for on this earth… naught is left to us but suffering. Oh, what an enviable lot!"; 28 Feb 1889, "Our father must be greatly loved by God, since he has so much to suffer. What a delight to share in his humiliation!"; 14 Jul 1889, God "has stricken a heavy blow, but it is through love". Later she wrote in HA: "The three years of my father's martyrdom seem to me the dearest and most fruitful of our life; I would not exchange them for the most sublime ecstasies" (HA ch. vii p.126). She calls 1889–94 "five long years of deathlike separation" (19 Aug 1894). The family quoted Victor Hugo's "A Villequier" (broken heart lines).

### F-LF-203 — Profession delayed; profession 8 September 1890
- Claim: At the end of her novitiate year (Jan 1890) she was not called to make her vows; Laveille says Delatroëtte, "still inflexible", opposed it. She prayed that she would wait as long as God pleased. The delay was "eight long months" beyond the usual term. Her retreat before profession was one of great aridity. On the night of 7 Sep, during the vigil, her vocation seemed "a dream, an illusion" and she wished to go back to the world; she told Mère Marie des Anges, who laughed heartily and reassured her. On the morning of 8 Sep 1890 the weather was radiant and "a veritable cloud of swallows" passed the monastery as the nuns went to the chapter room. Marie de Gonzague told her to ask, prostrate, for her father's cure; she prayed for it "if it be truly Thy Holy Will". She also asked that Léonie become a Visitation nun. She wrote: "a river of peace inundated my soul" (HA ch. viii p.134). Her canonical examination answer: "I have come to Carmel to save souls, and more especially to pray for priests." Louis, per FR Wikipedia, gave the Carmel 10,000 francs at her profession (single source).
- When / where: Jan–Sep 1890; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Delatroëtte; Mère Marie des Anges; Marie de Gonzague
- Source: LAV p.212, p.218, p.222–229 (HA ch. viii p.134)
- Source type: own-writing / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Laveille's swallows remark carries symbolism that is his comment.
### F-LF-204 — Veiling, 24 September 1890, without her father
- Claim: The public veiling ceremony was set for 24 Sep 1890. A slight improvement in Louis raised hope that he would come, but at the last moment those in charge feared strong emotion would worsen his illness, and he did not come. Thérèse "shed torrents of tears", and those around her were astonished at her weakness. She wrote to Céline on 23 Sep: "papa will not be here tomorrow… Jesus wants me to be an orphan… Tomorrow's feast will be for us a feast of tears." Laveille adds that Bishop Hugonin, expected to preside, did not come and "several other incidents filled the day with bitterness" (not named).
- When / where: 23–24 Sep 1890; Lisieux Carmel

### F-LF-205 — Jeanne's wedding and the mock invitation
- Claim: Jeanne Guérin married Dr Francis La Néele on 1 Oct 1890, eight days after the veiling. On the next parlour visit she spoke of the attentions she gave her husband. Thérèse said, "It shall not be said that a woman in the world will do more for her husband, an ordinary mortal, than I for my well-beloved Jesus." This led to her mock wedding announcement: God and the Virgin "announce… the spiritual nuptials of their august Son, Jesus… with little Thérèse Martin, now Queen and Princess of kingdoms given her as dowry… the Childhood of Jesus and His Passion", celebrated "on Mount Carmel on the 8 September, 1890 — the Celestial Court alone being admitted", and invites guests "to the home-coming… tomorrow, the day of Eternity".

### F-LF-206 — Fr Alexis Prou's retreat, 1891, and Marie de Gonzague's ban
- Claim: The 1891 retreat was preached by Fr Alexis (Prou), a Franciscan Recollect from Caen, known for converting workmen and servants. Thérèse feared he would find her difficulties scrupulous, but felt understood at the first interview. She wrote he "launched me under full sail on the waters of confidence and love… He told me that my failings did not displease the good God. 'I hold His place at this moment,' he added… 'He is well satisfied with your soul.'" Laveille says that, "by a regrettable abuse of authority", Marie de Gonzague forbade her to go back as others did to speak further, and Thérèse suffered in silence.

### F-LF-207 — Death of Mère Geneviève, 5 December 1891
- Claim: A few weeks before her death Mère Geneviève told Thérèse, "Serve God in peace and joy; remember, my child, that our God is the God of Peace" (a "spiritual bouquet"; Thérèse was desolate that day and was filled with joy). At the deathbed Thérèse felt "a sort of insensibility" for two hours, but "at the moment of her birth in heaven" her disposition changed and she was filled with joy (HA ch. viii p.138). A tear glistened on Mère Geneviève's eyelash; that evening Thérèse took it in a scrap of fine linen. As sacristan she arranged the flowers round the coffin. A lay sister said she put her family's wreaths in front and the bouquets of the poor behind. Thérèse said, "I am thankful to you, Sister. You are right. Give me the moss-covered cross sent by the workmen; I am going to place it in front" (Summarium 1919 p.559 §1544).
- When / where: Dec 1891; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Geneviève; Thérèse; an unnamed lay sister

### F-LF-209 — The influenza epidemic, winter 1891–92
- Claim: Laveille says influenza first appeared in France about 1890. In the last days of 1891 it struck the Carmel: all the sisters were attacked except two who escaped entirely, and Thérèse, who was only slightly affected. The very ill were nursed by those scarcely able to walk. Thérèse, "scarcely nineteen", alone in the sacristy, prepared funerals that took place "three times within a few days", and nursed the sick. One morning she felt a sister had died, went through the darkness to her cell and found her already laid on the bed, dead; she fetched a blessed candle and placed a crown of roses on her head (HA ch. viii p.140). Delatroëtte, visiting, saw her at work, "rid himself of all prejudice, and afterwards, with tears in his eyes, spoke in terms of admiration of her". During the epidemic the confessor allowed her daily Communion; it lasted some months after.
- When / where: Dec 1891 – early 1892; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Delatroëtte; the sisters; Abbé Youf
- Source: LAV p.240–241
- Source type: own-writing / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the story; the number of dead is not given in the pages read.
- Notes: Marie de Gonzague, "inflexible", had refused daily Communion (LAV p.241). Opponents' change: Delatroëtte's opposition ended.
### F-LF-210 — Louis returns from the Bon-Sauveur, 10 May 1892
- Claim: Louis left the asylum on 10 May 1892 after more than three years. Isidore Guérin fetched him. Mme Guérin wrote that he arrived at 4 p.m. and "his poor legs can no longer carry him. He had to be carried into the carriage. He wept all the time." He was put in a second-floor room in the Guérin house. Mme Guérin wrote on 15 May that he sat in the garden in the afternoons, was walked by two people, "wants all that we want", and had been taken to the Carmel on Thursday 12 May, where he knew everyone; the Carmelites wept afterwards. At the parting he raised his eyes and said "In heaven" (LAV p.247, no date).
- When / where: 10–12 May 1892; Lisieux
- People: Louis; Isidore and Céline Guérin; Céline; Léonie; the Carmelite daughters
- Source: Mme Guérin, 8, 10 and 15 May 1892 (Carmel archive; family-letters notes); LAV p.247; Carmel page rues Labbey et Paul-Banaston
- Source type: eyewitness (letters)

### F-LF-212 — Pauline becomes prioress; Thérèse helps with the novices
- Claim: In Feb 1893 Marie de Gonzague completed her term and the nuns elected Pauline (Sr Agnès de Jésus) prioress. Mère Agnès made Marie de Gonzague mistress of novices, because her experience and "certain material services" seemed to mark her, and, since the post needs "an evenness of disposition, sometimes lacking in the former Prioress", gave her an assistant, Thérèse, who was still in the novitiate. With Marie de Gonzague's consent, Thérèse formed first two lay sisters, "invested with the duties of novice-mistress without the corresponding title". Marie de Gonzague was prioress again in 1896 and kept the novice mistress's role, leaving Thérèse to form the novices under her control. Thérèse asked to remain in the novitiate, not among the professed, and there were soon five novices. Laveille says "four sisters" (Marie, Pauline, Céline and Thérèse) plus their cousin Marie Guérin, in a community of about twenty; Thérèse made "superhuman efforts" to keep union.
- When / where: 1893–1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Pauline (Mère Agnès); Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse; Marie Guérin
- Source: LAV p.278–279, p.283–284
- Source type: early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
