- Notes: Opponent/conflict: Marie de Gonzague's tendency to take offence.
### F-LF-213 — Léonie's second try at the Visitation, 1893–1895
- Claim: Léonie entered the Visitation at Caen a second time on 24 Jun 1893, aged thirty, against family advice to wait for Louis's death; postulant 6 Jul 1893; habit 6 Jul 1894 as Sr Thérèse-Dosithée. Isidore Guérin fetched her home on 20 Jul 1895. Her final entry: 28 Jan 1899 (postulant 1 Feb 1899; habit 30 Jun 1899; profession 2 Jul 1900) as Sr Françoise-Thérèse.
- When / where: 1893–1900; Caen
- People: Léonie; Isidore Guérin
- Source: Piat (1967), Carmel page https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/personnage/soeur-francoise-therese/ ; FR Wikipedia "Léonie Martin"
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: unknown / facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Céline says Léonie had left her in June 1893 to re-enter.
### F-LF-214 — Céline and the dance
- Claim: Laveille says that, at a wedding in Lisieux, Céline felt she could not refuse an invitation to a dance. Thérèse summoned her to the parlour and warned her with the three young Hebrews in the fiery furnace: would the spouse of Jesus "adore the world's golden statue by giving yourself to dangerous pleasures?" At the soirée her uncle brought her, and she refused for a long time, "even at the risk of offending". At last, "literally forced" (her words) by a young man, both found themselves "as though paralyzed" and could only walk with solemn steps. He led her back and "quietly slipped away much abashed, and did not dare to appear again". Céline credited Thérèse's prayers (Summarium 1919 p.400 §1013). Céline had already made a private vow of chastity.
- When / where: c. 1890–1894; Lisieux
- People: Céline; Thérèse; Isidore Guérin; an unnamed young man
- Source: LAV p.249–250
- Source type: eyewitness (Céline)
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — the year is not given; the account is Céline's.
- Notes: See M-LF-009 for the "extraordinary" reading. Surprise: Céline says she was "forced" to dance.
### F-LF-215 — Louis's death, 29 July 1894
- Claim: Louis died at La Musse on Sunday 29 Jul 1894 at about 8.15 a.m. Céline wrote to Carmel: "Papa est au Ciel !.. J'ai reçu son dernier soupir, je lui ai fermé les yeux." Extreme Unction had been given the previous evening. Isidore said he had never seen so peaceful a death. Céline saw a luminous globe in the sky in the night. The body stayed until 2 Aug. Laveille says he gave Céline "a last look of loving gratitude". Céline (1909) says she was alone with him until Mme Guérin came, then went to fetch Isidore. Thérèse wrote to Céline (19 Aug 1894) that "Our dear father is making us feel his presence… he has not yet been a month in heaven, and already… all your projects succeed."
- When / where: 29 Jul 1894, 8.15 a.m.; La Musse
- People: Louis; Céline; Isidore and Céline Guérin
- Source: Céline's letter of 29 Jul 1894 (Carmel archive; family-letters notes); Mme Guérin's letter (EWTN excerpt, https://www.ewtn.com/catholicism/library/her-father-louis-martin-13782); LAV p.250–252
- Source type: eyewitness
- Rights: unknown / public-domain
- Original words: « Papa est au Ciel !.. J'ai reçu son dernier soupir, je lui ai fermé les yeux »
- Translation: Papa is in Heaven! I received his last breath; I closed his eyes.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The "luminous globe" is Céline's report (see M-LF-011). The funeral at Saint-Pierre and burial: F-LF-216.
### F-LF-216 — Funeral and the reburial at Lisieux, October 1894
- Claim: Louis was buried at Lisieux (Saint-Pierre funeral). Laveille's footnote says that after Louis's burial at Lisieux in 1894 Isidore moved Zélie, the dead children and the grandparents ("M. Guérin, Mme Martin") from Alençon to the Lisieux cemetery. The handoff notes the date as 10 Oct 1894. The Carmel wording is "grand-mère Guérin et grand-père Martin".
- When / where: Aug and 10 Oct 1894; Lisieux; Alençon
- People: Isidore Guérin; Louis; Zélie; the dead children
- Source: LAV p.95 fn.; Carmel notes (see sources.md)
- Source type: early-life / scholarship
- Rights: public-domain / unknown
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium
- Notes: CONFLICT: which grandparents were moved (Laveille vs Carmel): the words differ (whose parents). See gaps file.
### F-LF-217 — Céline enters Carmel, 14 September 1894
- Claim: Whether four sisters could be together in one Carmel was debated. Marie de Gonzague, "then mistress of novices and Council Sister", strongly urged Mère Agnès to allow it. One nun openly opposed, fearing a precedent. Thérèse prayed to her dead father, after Communion, that the obstacle be removed as a sign that he had gone straight to heaven, and just after thanksgiving she met the nun, who told her earnestly she would be happy to see Céline. Thérèse wrote to Céline: "I am not ill; I am strong as iron; but God can break iron as easily as potter's clay." Céline entered on 14 Sep 1894, as Sr Geneviève de la Sainte-Face, "not quite a month later" than Louis's death.
- When / where: Aug–Sep 1894; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Céline; Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Marie de Gonzague; an unnamed opposing nun
- Source: LAV p.250–252
- Source type: early-life / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: Opponent type: a nun; reason: precedent (Laveille does not name her).

## Part 9 — The last years in Carmel (1894–1897)
### F-LF-218 — Ms A: ordered in December 1894, handed over 20 January 1896
- Claim: Laveille says that one winter evening in December 1894, at recreation in the community room ("the only place where the nuns had permission to warm themselves"), Mère Agnès (then prioress), Marie and Thérèse sat round the fire and Thérèse told tales of her childhood. Marie took the prioress aside: "It is really a pity that… she writes nothing that will preserve to us the memories of her childhood. Our little sister is an angel who will not long remain on earth." Mère Agnès waited some weeks and then ordered Thérèse to write an account of her childhood for her feast day. She began at the start of 1895, writing in rare leisure moments (her free time went to the sacristy). On 20 Jan 1896, going to evening prayer, she knelt and gave the prioress the manuscript, "written without erasure on poor paper that a servant would have disdained to use". Mère Agnès nodded, laid it on her stall, and did not read it for months, until she was no longer prioress. Thérèse showed no anxiety. Later Mère Agnès persuaded Marie de Gonzague (prioress again) to order a second part on her life in Carmel (chapters IX–X); the pages of her 1896 retreat, to Marie, became chapter XI.
- When / where: Dec 1894 – 1896; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Agnès; Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.367–368
- Source type: early-life / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- Other sources: Carmel page "la fabrication de l'Histoire d'une âme" https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/naissance-dune-sainte/lhistoire-du-ame/la-fabrication-de-lhistoire-dune-ame/ (via helper): the Carmel gives 30 May 1897 and 2–4 Jun 1897 for Ms C.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Own-writings family will cover the texts. The Carmel explains that Ms C began because Mère Agnès saw the illness was far advanced and a short obituary circular (about three pages) would not do. The "one addressee" issue: F-LF-241.
### F-OM-009 — The documents link her religious name to "spiritual childhood"
- Claim: The brief says that at her profession (8 Sep 1890) she chose to be called "of the Child Jesus", as if to signify the kind of holiness she pursued, "the perfection called spiritual childhood". Benedict XV said that if the title had not fallen to her, her sisters would have given it to her, and quoted her: "I am called Thérèse of the Child Jesus because the Child Jesus is the master and model of Thérèse" (as he gives it).
- When / where: 8 Sep 1890
- Source: AAS 15 p. 204; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 292
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Theresia a Puero Iesu maluit vocari »
- Notes: The baseline says she took the name on entering (1888/1889). The brief ties the choice to profession. This may be the brief's simplification. See gaps file.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium
### F-OM-010 — Named assistant novice mistress at twenty by her own sister
- Claim: The brief says that three years after her profession the prioress, Agnès of Jesus, her own sister, judged she could not do better than put Thérèse over the novices with the title and office of assistant mistress; so at twenty she took up this heavy task and kept it until death, being "mother and sister" to them.
- When / where: 1893, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline)
- Source: AAS 15 pp. 204–205
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
### F-OW-086 — Clothing day, 10 Jan 1889: the snow
- Claim: She had always wished nature to be white on her clothing day; the day was mild and grey with fine rain. Her father "had never been more handsome, more dignified... it was his last feast here below". The bishop intoned the Te Deum though a priest pointed out it was only for professions. Going back into the cloister she saw her "pink little Jesus" among flowers and then snow falling: the courtyard was white. "The whole town was astonished"; people found her "odd taste" for snow.
- When / where: 10 Jan 1889, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; Mgr Hugonin
- Source: Ms A 72r–72v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « le préau était blanc comme moi »
- Translation: "the courtyard was white like me"
- Other sources: See M-OW-05.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-087 — 12 Feb 1889: "the most humiliating of all cups"
- Claim: "On 12 February, a month after my clothing, our dear Father would drink the most bitter, the most humiliating of all cups." "On that day I did not say I could suffer more!" She calls the three years of her father's "martyrdom" "the most lovable, the most fruitful of our whole life; I would not give them for all the ecstasies and revelations of the saints." She also calls Léonie and Céline "the two poor little exiles of Caen".
- When / where: 12 Feb 1889 (Louis committed to the Bon-Sauveur asylum, Caen, per other sources)
- People: Louis Martin; Léonie; Céline
- Source: Ms A 73r–73v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « notre Père chéri boirait à la plus amère, à la plus humiliante de toutes les coupes »
- Translation: "our dear Father would drink from the most bitter, the most humiliating of all cups"
- Other sources: The 1911 text keeps "calice aussi amer" but drops "la plus humiliante" and "les deux pauvres petites exilées de Caen" (HA 1911 ch. VII).
- New to English readers: yes ("most humiliating", Caen)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She never names the asylum or the illness.
### F-OW-088 — Leading Sr Saint-Pierre to the refectory
- Claim: As a novice she offered to lead the old, crippled Sr Saint-Pierre from evening prayer to the refectory at ten to six. The sister shook her hourglass to mean "let's go". The bench had to be moved "in a certain way"; Thérèse held her by the belt. "Ah, my God! you're going too fast, I'm going to break." Slower: "But follow me! I can't feel your hand, you've let go of me, I'm going to fall; ah, I said you were too young to lead me." At table Thérèse rolled up her sleeves "in a certain way" and, unasked, cut her bread; she won her over by her "most beautiful smile". One winter night she heard distant music and imagined a lit salon with elegant girls, and looked at the moaning old nun and the brick cloister: "to enjoy worldly feasts for a thousand years I would not have given the ten minutes" of this service.
- When / where: c. 1889–1890, cloister and refectory, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Saint-Pierre de Sainte-Thérèse (old infirm nun)
- Source: Ms C 28v–30r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ah! mon Dieu! vous allez trop vite, j'vais m'briser. »
- Translation: "Oh my God, you're going too fast, I'm going to break!"
- New to English readers: partial (in Taylor, not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-090 — Profession delayed eight months; working in the refectory with Pauline
- Claim: After a year the prioress told her not to ask for profession because the superior would refuse; she had to wait eight months more. She recognised "a great self-love" in her wish, and resolved to make "a beautiful dress" of virtues first. Her first job after clothing was the refectory, with Pauline; she could not speak freely to her because of the Rule: "I was in Carmel and no longer at Les Buissonnets."
- When / where: 1889–1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline); Canon Delatroëtte (superior)
- Source: Ms A 73v–75r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « enfin j'étais au Carmel et non plus aux Buissonnets »
- Translation: "after all I was in Carmel and no longer at Les Buissonnets"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-092 — Fleeing like a deserter: the fight over the keys
- Claim: As sacristan, during one of the prioress's bronchitis attacks, she went to return the keys of the communion grille. A zealous young sister (senior to her in religion) tried to take the keys so she would not wake the prioress; Thérèse, "too sly to give them up", insisted, the other pushed the door, and the noise woke the prioress. The sister made "a whole speech": "It's Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus who made the noise... my God, how disagreeable she is!" Unable to stay silent virtuously, Thérèse fled "without drum or trumpet"; her heart beat so hard she sat down on the stairs "to enjoy in peace the fruits of my victory". "It was not bravery, was it, Mother?"
- When / where: c. 1890–1892, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; an unnamed young sister (identified by editors as Sr Marthe de Jésus? not stated)
- Source: Ms C 14v–15r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je partis sans tambour ni trompette »
- Translation: "I left without drum or trumpet" (she slipped away quietly)
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-093 — Profession retreat: the "underground passage" and the honeymoon with no view
- Claim: In her retreat before profession, Thérèse told Pauline that Jesus had led her into an "underground passage", neither cold nor hot, with no sun, rain or wind. The only light came from the lowered eyes of Christ's Face. She and her "Fiancé" said nothing to each other. She could not see any progress. She said the road gave her no consolation, and yet it gave her all consolations because Jesus chose it.
- When / where: 30–31 Aug 1890, Carmel of Lisieux, during the retreat before her profession on 8 Sept
- People: Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline Martin), her sister, whom she calls "Petite Maman"
- Source: LT 110 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 30–31 Aug 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-110-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-30-31-aout-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Il m'a fait entrer dans un souterrain où il ne fait ni froid ni chaud, où le soleil ne luit pas et que la pluie ni le vent ne visitent pas »
- Translation: "He made me go into an underground passage where it is neither cold nor hot, where the sun does not shine, and where neither rain nor wind comes."
- Other sources: LT 111 (to Marie): "Son voyage de noces est bien aride… la nuit lui empêche de rien admirer". LT 112: "je suis dans un souterrain bien obscur". LT 115: "Mon âme est toujours dans le souterrain mais elle y est bien heureuse".
- New to English readers: partial. The 1911 edition prints it as "Lettre IVe" to Mère Agnès, but that letter is a composite of LT 110, LT 112 and LT 115, and it is reworded (see the edit card).
- Confidence: high. Her own text.
- Notes: The same letter also has practical questions: « Faut-il écrire à M. Le pelletier et à M. Révérony que je fais profession ?... » ("Should I write to M. Lepelletier and M. Révérony that I am making profession?").
### F-OW-094 — Pauline's little glass of wine, and permission from Mother Prioress
- Claim: Thérèse reminded Pauline to go to the cellar to take her "little wine". She herself was taking a "little wine" during the retreat. On 1 Sept she wrote that she would take hers, and that she would gladly have had it that morning, but she could not find "Notre Mère" (the prioress, whose permission she needed).
- When / where: 30–31 Aug and 1 Sept 1890, Carmel
- People: Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline); "Notre Mère", the prioress, Mère Marie de Gonzague (the letter does not give her name)

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Surtout n'oubliez pas d'aller dans la cave prendre votre petit vin » ; « (Je vais prendre mon petit vin, le même ce matin aurait été bien de mon goût mais je n'ai pas pu trouver Notre Mère). »
- Translation: "Above all, do not forget to go to the cellar and take your little wine." / "(I am going to take my little wine. The same this morning would have been very much to my taste, but I could not find Our Mother.)"

### F-OW-095 — A letter for her sick father "who will not understand"
- Claim: Thérèse gave Pauline a letter she had written for their father. She asked Pauline to make a draft for her if it would not do, "but I really think he will not understand". On 4 Sept she sent "the letter from Rome" (the papal blessing) for Céline to take to him. She said, "if sometimes he understood, he would be so happy". She asked whether to send him her vows to bless, placed "in the middle of the crown".
- When / where: 1 and 4 Sept 1890. Louis Martin was then in the asylum at Caen (the Bon Sauveur). The letters say only "Papa".

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Je vous donne la lettre que j'ai écrite pour Papa ; si vous trouvez qu'elle ne peut pas aller, vous allez bien vouloir me faire un petit brouillon, mais je crois bien qu'il ne comprendra pas... » ; « Peut-être Papa ne comprendra-t-il pas, mais cela n'est pas difficile à emporter et si quelquefois il comprenait il serait si heureux. »
- Translation: "I am giving you the letter I wrote for Papa. If you think it will not do, please make me a little draft, but I really think he will not understand…" / "Perhaps Papa will not understand, but it is not hard to take there, and if sometimes he understood he would be so happy."

### F-OW-096 — Confession before profession: a "little review" with M. Youf
- Claim: Before profession, Thérèse was to see M. Youf the next day. He told her to make "a little review" only of the time since she entered the Carmel. She was very happy to have received absolution on Saturday. She did not understand her retreat and "thought of nothing".

### F-OW-097 — "Do you think Céline is really going to die?"
- Claim: Five days before her profession, Thérèse asked Pauline whether Céline was really going to die. She had promised Céline the day before to make profession "for both of us". ### F-OW-098 — Asking to die on profession day if she would offend God afterwards
- Claim: Thérèse asked Pauline to tell Jesus to take her on her profession day if she was going to offend Him after it. She wanted to carry to heaven "the white robe of my second baptism" without stain. She also wrote that she wanted, not "sensible" love, but love "felt by Jesus".

### F-OW-099 — Profession practicalities: sewing, notices and the papal blessing
- Claim: Sr Thérèse de Jésus gave Thérèse a note asking her to do some work. Thérèse did not have patterns, and she thought "the linen and the Blessed Virgin" were more urgent. ### F-OW-100 — Night before profession: "I did not have a vocation!"
- Claim: The evening before profession, doing the Stations of the Cross after Matins, "my vocation appeared to me as a dream, a chimera"; "the devil" told her Carmel was not for her and she would deceive her superiors. Mère Geneviève told her later she had had the same trial.

- Original words: « Je n'avais pas la vocation !... »
- Translation: "I did not have a vocation!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-101 — The faded pink candles from Papa
- Claim: For her profession, Thérèse asked Marie to light the candles of the Child Jesus statue before she went to chapter. She did not use the new pink candles. She used the old ones, which had first burned on her clothing day, when Papa (who had given them) was present. Now "the pink colour has gone". She called herself "the orphan of the Berezina".
- When / where: 7 Sept 1890, eve of profession, Carmel
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Marie Martin, her godmother); Louis Martin
- Source: LT 116 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 7 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-116-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-7-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « elles ont commencé à brûler le jour de ma prise d'habit, alors elles étaient roses et fraîches. Papa (qui me les avait données) était là et tout était joie !... mais maintenant la couleur de rose est partie, y a-t-il encore pour l'orpheline de la Beresina des joies couleur de rose ici-bas ? »
- Translation: "They began to burn on my clothing day. Then they were pink and fresh. Papa (who had given them to me) was there and all was joy!… But now the pink colour has gone. Are there still rose-coloured joys here below for the orphan of the Berezina?"
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre Ve (7 Sept 1890) to Marie
- New to English readers: partial. The candles are in 1911, but 1911 cuts "l'orpheline de la Beresina" (ABSENT) and changes the addressee's words.
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "Beresina" refers to the 1812 retreat from Russia, used here as a family joke about being an orphan. The letter does not explain it.
### F-OW-102 — Profession, 8 Sep 1890; asleep at prayer for seven years
- Claim: She made vows in peace "like the light breeze" Elijah heard; "I felt truly queen" and asked to free souls from purgatory and convert sinners; she prayed especially "for my little Father, so tried and so holy". Her retreats were "of great aridity". "I should be desolate that I have slept (for 7 years) during my prayers and thanksgivings; well, I am not desolate... little children please their parents as much asleep as awake... doctors put their patients to sleep to operate."
- When / where: 8 Sep 1890; reflection 1895
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 75v–76v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je devrais me désoler de dormir (depuis 7 ans) pendant mes oraisons et mes actions de grâces ; eh bien, je ne me désole pas »
- Translation: "I ought to be upset that I have been falling asleep (for 7 years) during my prayer and thanksgiving; well, I'm not upset."
- Other sources: The sleeping passage is absent from the 1911 edited text.
- New to English readers: yes (the sleeping confession was cut)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-103 — The wedding invitation: Louis Martin, "master of the lordships of suffering"
- Claim: Thérèse wrote a mock wedding announcement. God and the Virgin Mary announce the marriage of their Son Jesus to "Mademoiselle Thérèse Martin". A second paragraph has "Monsieur Louis Martin, owner and master of the Lordships of suffering and humiliation", and "Madame Martin, princess and lady of honour of the heavenly court", announcing their daughter's marriage.

### F-OW-105 — Eve of the veiling: Papa will not come
- Claim: On 23 Sept 1890, the eve of her public veiling, Thérèse wrote to Céline. The joy of seeing their father at the ceremony had been refused. Her tears were still flowing and she could "hardly hold my pen". She said "another" would explain the talk in "my uncle's parlour", but she could only speak "the language of Heaven". She quoted a letter from "the exiled Father": "Neither of your fathers will be there to offer you to Jesus."
- When / where: 23 Sept 1890, Carmel; the veiling was the next day
- People: Céline; Isidore Guérin, her uncle (his "parloir"); "le Père exilé" (identity not given in the letter, but likely Fr Almire Pichon SJ, then in Canada, from outside knowledge); Louis Martin
- Source: LT 120 (to Céline, 23 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-120-a-celine-23-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « je puis à peine tenir ma plume... et puis une autre te ferait beaucoup d'explications du parloir de mon oncle, mais ta Thérèse ne sait te parler que le langage du Ciel » ; « Ni l'un ni l'autre de vos pères ne sera là pour vous offrir à Jésus. »
- Translation: "I can hardly hold my pen… Another girl would give you many explanations about the talk in my uncle's parlour, but your Thérèse can only speak to you the language of Heaven." / "Neither of your fathers will be there to offer you to Jesus."
- Other sources: Ms A tells of her tears on the veiling day. 1911 Lettre IXe keeps the core of the letter.
- New to English readers: partial. The core is in 1911, but the uncle's parlour and the Pichon quotation are cut.
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter implies, but does not state, that the decision was made in the uncle's parlour.
- Notes: "Ni l'un ni l'autre de vos pères": her father and her director could not come.
### F-OW-106 — EDIT: 1911 adds "Papa ne viendra pas demain!" to LT 120
- Claim: The original never says in plain words that Papa will not come. It speaks of a "diamond" Jesus gave her "today". The 1911 text adds the sentence « Papa ne viendra pas demain! ». It also moves "my tears are still flowing" to "while I write to you". It cuts the uncle's parlour, the "exiled Father's" letter, and "our spouse is a spouse of tears and not of smiles".

- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (editor)

- Source type: own-writing compared with the edited text
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: LT 120: « ce diamant précieux Jésus me l'a donné aujourd'hui... Céline... en le recevant mes larmes ont coulé » / 1911: « ce diamant précieux, Jésus me l'a donné aujourd'hui... Papa ne viendra pas demain! »
- Translation: "this precious diamond Jesus gave me today… Céline… as I received it my tears flowed" / 1911: "…Papa will not come tomorrow!"
- Other sources: chk.py "papa ne viendra pas": FOUND in 1911 only; "le Père exilé": ABSENT
- New to English readers: yes (the difference itself)
- Confidence: high

### F-OW-107 — Card for Sr Marthe de Jésus, her "little companion"
- Claim: On 23 Sept 1890 Thérèse gave a short keepsake to Sr Marthe de Jésus, "my dear little Companion", for "the most beautiful day of your life", the day she was consecrated to Jesus. She signed as "unworthy little Sister".

- People: Sr Marthe de Jésus, a fellow novice

### F-OW-108 — Taking of the veil, 24 Sep 1890: "all veiled in tears"
- Claim: The day "was all veiled in tears": her father was not there to bless her (Céline's plan to bring him to the grille was blocked by "human prudence"); Fr Pichon was in Canada; the bishop was ill and did not come. "That day Jesus let me be unable to hold back my tears, and my tears were not understood."
- When / where: 24 Sep 1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; Fr Pichon; Mgr Hugonin
- Source: Ms A 75r–75v; 77r

### F-OW-109 — Formal letter after profession: "enemies of the Church" in Rome
- Claim: Thérèse wrote a formal letter to Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix. She said she was now "all Jesus's" and had received the Holy Father's blessing for her profession. The religious who obtained it wrote how many the enemies of the Church were, and that in Rome "the struggle never stops for a moment" against the Pope. She asked for prayers for her father, "so tried by the cross and so admirable in his resignation".
- When / where: 28 Sept 1890, "Monastère du Carmel"
- People: Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix (of another community; which one is not named); Louis Martin; Pope Leo XIII (as "le Saint Père")
- Source: LT 121 (to Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix, 28 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-121-a-soeur-marie-joseph-de-la-croix-28-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « à Rome la lutte ne cesse pas un instant à l'égard de notre Saint Père le Pape. C'est désolant... »
- Translation: "In Rome the struggle against our Holy Father the Pope does not stop for a moment. It is distressing…"
- Other sources: LT 114–115 (the "letter from Rome")
- New to English readers: yes ("les ennemis de l'Eglise": ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is a rare political remark. It reports what her Roman correspondent wrote.
### F-OW-110 — Her mock wedding invitation (after Jeanne Guérin's marriage)
- Claim: Eight days after her veiling, her cousin Jeanne married Francis La Néele; watching Jeanne's attentions to her husband, Thérèse wanted to do no less for Jesus. For fun she composed an "Invitation to the Wedding of Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus of the Holy Face", announcing "Monsieur Louis Martin, Owner and Master of the Lordships of Suffering and Humiliation" and inviting guests to the "Return from the Wedding... tomorrow, the Day of Eternity... the hour being still uncertain, you are invited to be ready and to watch".
- When / where: Oct 1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Jeanne Guérin; Francis La Néele; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 77r–77v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Monsieur Louis Martin, Propriétaire et Maître des Seigneuries de la Souffrance et de l'Humiliation »
- Translation: "Monsieur Louis Martin, Proprietor and Lord of the Manors of Suffering and Humiliation"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-111 — "I do not see the Sacred Heart like everyone else"
- Claim: Writing to Céline, who was on a journey "là-bas", Thérèse said she did not see the Sacred Heart as everyone else did. She thought of her Spouse's heart as hers alone, as hers was His alone. She asked Céline to murmur her name "over there". She could not write to Léonie: "I don't even have time to reread myself". She signed "nov.carm.ind." (unworthy Carmelite novice), even after profession.
- When / where: 14 Oct 1890. The journey's destination is not named; the Sacred Heart link suggests Paray-le-Monial, but that is not stated.
- People: Céline; Léonie; Marie (the letter was added to "la lettre de Marie")
- Source: LT 122 (to Céline, 14 Oct 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-122-a-celine-14-octobre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « moi je ne vois pas le Sacré Cœur comme tout le monde, je pense que le cœur de mon époux est à moi seul comme le mien est à lui seul »
- Translation: "I do not see the Sacred Heart like everyone else. I think that my Spouse's heart is mine alone, as mine is His alone."
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre Xe (14 Oct 1890) is a heavy rewrite of this letter.
- New to English readers: yes ("je ne vois pas le Sacré": ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The same letter says "Our Mother" allowed her to "come and make my prayer with you". See the edit card for LT 122.
### F-OW-112 — EDIT: 1911 rewrites LT 122 and adds a St John of the Cross verse
- Claim: The 1911 "Lettre Xe" keeps little of LT 122. It opens with a new sentence, « Je comprends tout ce que tu souffres, je comprends tes déchirements et je les partage ». It changes "des millions d'années" to "des millions de siècles, pour toujours". It then adds a five-line verse from St John of the Cross ("Le visage incliné sur mon Bien-Aimé…") that is not in LT 122. It cuts the Sacred Heart passage, the lines on virginity and "the friends of Jesus are rare" among priests, and the message to Léonie.

- Source type: own-writing compared with the edited text
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: LT 122: « des millions d'années passeront pour nous comme un jour » / 1911: « pour des millions de siècles, pour toujours! »
- Translation: "millions of years will pass for us like one day" / "for millions of centuries, for ever!"
- Other sources: 1911 text read directly
- New to English readers: yes (the difference)

### F-OW-113 — Feast-day presents: heather pots and cakes from the Guérins
- Claim: For her feast (15 Oct), the Guérins sent Marie Guérin to wish her a happy feast. Jeanne and Marie sent two pots of heather, now placed near the Child Jesus. Her aunt sent "delicious cakes". Thérèse knew her aunt was ill. She also prayed to comfort her aunt for "the great emptiness" left by Jeanne's departure (Jeanne had just married).
- When / where: 15 Oct 1890
- People: Mme Céline Guérin (née Fournet), her aunt; Jeanne Guérin (newly Mme Francis La Néele); Marie Guérin; Isidore Guérin

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Les deux jolis pots de fleurs… sont placés auprès du petit Jésus et à chaque heure du jour réclament pour mes deux petites sœurs autant de grâces et de bénédictions que chaque bruyère contient de petites fleurs »
- Translation: "The two pretty flowerpots… are placed near the little Jesus, and at every hour of the day they ask, for my two little sisters, as many graces and blessings as each heather has little flowers."
- Other sources: LT 125 and LT 133 (her aunt's feast); LT 135 (she asks Céline for heather)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She calls her cousins "my two little sisters". This shows how close the family ties were.

- Claim: From profession on, Thérèse signs "rel.carm.ind." ("unworthy Carmelite religious"). In LT 122 and LT 127 (Oct 1890, Apr 1891) she still signs "nov.carm.ind." (unworthy Carmelite novice). One 1893 note (LT 153) is signed "rel.carm." without "ind.".

### F-OW-115 — Wishing Céline might die within the year
- Claim: For Céline's feast, Thérèse wrote that it might be "the last time" Céline's feast was kept on earth: "perhaps!… what a sweet hope". She sent two "Céline" flowers (a flower that shares Céline's name), one stem, one sun, "and no doubt the same day will see them die".
- When / where: 20 Oct 1890
- People: Céline
- Source: LT 124 (to Céline, 20 Oct 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-124-a-celine-20-octobre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Céline, c'est peut-être la dernière fois que ta fête sera fêtée sur la terre !... peut-être !... quel doux espoir... »
- Translation: "Céline, perhaps this is the last time your feast will be kept on earth!… perhaps!… what a sweet hope…"
- Other sources: LT 132 (Céline flowers blooming early); LT 130 (asking Jesus to take the "lilies" out of the world early)
- New to English readers: yes (not among the 1911 Céline letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is a strong example of her longing for heaven at 17, applied to her sister. It does not fit the usual sweet picture.
### F-OW-116 — Aunt Guérin's feast: "the right of a Benjamin"
- Claim: For her aunt's feast on 19 Nov, Thérèse called herself the "last, smallest" daughter, who would never be outdone in tenderness, "the right of a Benjamin". She remembered looking forward to 19 Nov as a child because of the treats ("gâteries"). Being "very poor", she charged her rich royal Spouse to pay her debt.
- When / where: 17 Nov 1890
- People: Mme Guérin
- Source: LT 125 (to Mme Guérin, 17 Nov 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-125-a-mme-guerin-17-novembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « n'est-ce pas le droit d'un Benjamin d'aimer plus que les autres ?... » ; « à cause des jolies gâteries dont j'étais comblée ce jour-là »
- Translation: "Is it not a Benjamin's right to love more than the others?…" / "because of the pretty treats I was showered with on that day"
- Other sources: LT 139, LT 152 ("Votre petit Benjamin, qui est fier de son titre"); LT 181 (Marie Guérin is now "notre Benjamin")
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The "Benjamin" title runs through these letters until Marie Guérin enters in 1895.
### F-OW-117 — Lending a book, in secret, to a doubting married cousin
- Claim: After a visit from "Marguerite M.", who told the sisters intimate secrets and whose faith was "in danger", Thérèse asked Céline to lend her a book on "the mysteries of the future life". Marguerite could read books without her husband knowing. Céline should seem not to know the book. She should tell Marguerite to begin at chapter 3, "where there is a little picture". Mme Maudelonde and "my aunt" should not know. The idea "was given to me" by God, but "Th. can do nothing without Céline".
- When / where: 3 Apr 1891; the visit was that afternoon in the Carmel parlour
- People: "Marguerite M." (the file gives only this; the link with Mme Maudelonde suggests Marguerite Maudelonde, a cousin, but her married name is not given); her unnamed husband; Mme Maudelonde; Mme Guérin; Céline
- Source: LT 126 (to Céline, 3 Apr 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-126-a-celine-3-avril-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Elle nous a dit qu'elle pouvait lire des livres sans que son mari le sache » ; « de le commencer seulement au 3e chapitre où il y a une petite image »
- Translation: "She told us she could read books without her husband knowing." / "to begin it only at the 3rd chapter, where there is a little picture"
- Other sources: none in this file. The title is not given. The phrase "les mystères de la vie future" matches the subtitle of Arminjon's book, which Ms A says she loved, but the letter does not name the book.
- New to English readers: yes ("Marguerite" in this sense: ABSENT)
- Confidence: high for the facts in the letter. Low for the book's identity.
- Notes: This is a small, discreet apostolate carried out through Céline. It shows her practical sense.
### F-OW-118 — "Together we are 40 years old now"
- Claim: For Céline's 22nd birthday, Thérèse wrote her fourth birthday letter from the Carmel. She asked why Céline never asked, "Why did God not create me an angel?" She said God wanted "angels of the earth". She hoped Céline would win "a certain faded and withered Lily" back to God. She added a joke: "together we are 40 years old now", so no wonder they had so much experience.
- When / where: 26 Apr 1891
- People: Céline; the "Lys fané" (not named here; see the Loyson card for LT 129)
- Source: LT 127 (to Céline, 26 Apr 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-127-a-celine-26-avril-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « (Sais-tu qu'à nous deux nous avons 40 ans maintenant, ce n'est pas étonnant que nous ayons déjà l'expérience de tant de choses, qu'en penses-tu ?) »
- Translation: "(Do you know that between the two of us we are 40 years old now? No wonder we already have experience of so many things. What do you think?)"
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre XIe (26 Apr 1891)
- New to English readers: partial. The "angel" passage is in 1911. The joke and the "faded Lily" are cut (ABSENT).
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See the edit card on 1911 Lettre XIe.

### F-OW-120 — Keepsake for Marie leaving the novitiate
- Claim: Thérèse gave Marie a keepsake "on the feast of the Precious Blood for her leaving the novitiate".

- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Marie Martin)

### F-OW-121 — Praying for the ex-priest Hyacinthe Loyson: "the unhappy prodigal"
- Claim: Thérèse gave Céline news of "the unhappy prodigal". He had gone to Coutances, where he repeated the lectures he had given at Caen, and he planned to tour France. People said remorse was eating at him. He went round churches with a large crucifix, and "his wife follows him everywhere". She said he was guilty, perhaps more than any sinner who was ever converted. But could Jesus not do once what He had never done? She quoted Christ's words to St Margaret Mary: "a just soul… can obtain pardon for a thousand criminals".
- When / where: 8 July 1891
- People: the ex-Carmelite Hyacinthe Loyson (not named in the letter, and outside knowledge identifies him); his wife; Céline; "Notre Mère" (ill)
- Source: LT 129 (to Céline, 8 July 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-129-a-celine-8-juillet-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Le malheureux prodigue est allé à Coutances où il a recommencé les conférences de Caen » ; « Sa femme le suit partout. »
- Translation: "The unhappy prodigal has gone to Coutances, where he started the Caen lectures again." / "His wife follows him everywhere."
- Other sources: LT 127 ("un certain Lys fané et flétri"); Ms C (she offered her last Communion for him, which is outside this file)
- New to English readers: yes ("le malheureux prodigue": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high for the words. Medium for the identification, which rests on outside knowledge and the context (a married former religious giving lectures).
- Notes: She calls him "notre frère, un fils de la Ste Vierge" ("our brother, a son of the Blessed Virgin").
### F-OW-122 — "Our Mother" is still very ill (July 1891)
- Claim: Thérèse told Céline that "Our dear Mother" was still very ill. It was sad to see those we love suffer, but Jesus would not refuse to leave her with them a while longer.
- When / where: 8 July 1891
- People: "Notre Mère chérie". The prioress in July 1891 was Mère Marie de Gonzague (outside knowledge), but the letter gives no name.
- Source: LT 129, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-129-a-celine-8-juillet-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Notre Mère chérie est encore bien souffrante, c'est bien triste de voir ainsi souffrir ceux que l'on aime. »
- Translation: "Our dear Mother is still very unwell. It is very sad to see those we love suffer like this."
- Other sources: LT 130 (Mère Geneviève's prayers)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium. The identity of the sick "Mother" is not stated.
- Notes: This shows tenderness towards the prioress, whom later accounts often cast as a hard figure.
### F-OW-123 — Mère Geneviève's feast: fish, cherries and cakes
- Claim: "Yesterday's feast was delightful, a real foretaste of Heaven." The Guérins' gifts (fish, cherries, cakes) pleased them. Mère Geneviève was touched by Céline's letter and prayed for her. Thérèse called her "such a saint".
- When / where: letter 23 July 1891; the feast was 22 July 1891
- People: Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse (a founding nun of the Lisieux Carmel); Céline; Mme Guérin
- Source: LT 130, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-130-a-celine-23-juillet-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Tous les cadeaux nous ont fait grand plaisir, le poisson, les cerises, les gâteaux »
- Translation: "All the presents gave us great pleasure: the fish, the cherries, the cakes."
- Other sources: LT 134 (a flower held by "our holy Mother Geneviève", used as a relic); LT 140 (Pauline wrote her life)
- New to English readers: yes

- Notes: This is food detail from Carmel life.
### F-OW-124 — The lawyer who came to ask for Céline's hand
- Claim: An "avoué" (a lawyer) had come to seek Céline in marriage. Thérèse wrote that he "made me laugh a lot". "He is not shy," to come for the fiancée of the King of Heaven. The poor man had not seen "the sign the Spouse has placed on your forehead".
- When / where: 23 July 1891
- People: Céline; an unnamed avoué
- Source: LT 130 (to Céline, 23 July 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-130-a-celine-23-juillet-1891/

- Original words: « L'avoué m'a bien fait rire, il faut avouer qu'il n'est pas gêné de venir chercher la fiancée du Roi du Ciel »
- Translation: "The lawyer made me laugh a lot. You have to admit he is not shy, coming to look for the fiancée of the King of Heaven."
- Other sources: none in this file
- New to English readers: yes ("L'avoué": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: There is a pun: « L'avoué… il faut avouer ». This is a real suitor, seen with humour.
### F-OW-125 — Papa's white hair turning into suns

### F-OW-126 — The 1891 retreat: "wondering whether there was a Heaven"
- Claim: At the retreat a year after her profession (Oct 1891), she had "great interior trials of all kinds (even to wondering sometimes whether there was a Heaven)". The preacher, meant for "great sinners", was "appreciated only by me": "my soul was like a book in which the Father read better than I did"; he told her that her faults did not grieve God and that God was very pleased with her. "I am of such a nature that fear makes me draw back; with love, not only do I go forward, I fly."
- When / where: Oct 1891, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Fr Alexis Prou OFM (not named in the text)
- Source: Ms A 80r–80v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « jusqu'à me demander parfois s'il y avait un Ciel »
- Translation: "even to asking myself at times whether there was a Heaven"
- Other sources: The phrase is absent from the 1911 text. Baseline (Wikipedia) names Fr Prou's retreat.
- New to English readers: yes (the early doubt about Heaven, 1891, before the 1896 trial)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Important: a first doubt about Heaven five years before the famous trial of faith.
### F-OW-127 — Jeanne and Francis La Néele: the pharmacy that would not sell
- Claim: Thanking Jeanne and Francis for her feast wishes, Thérèse prayed for a little "Isidore" or a little "Jeanne". She also prayed "that the pharmacy may at last be sold". She was willing to take on herself the trials God kept for those she loved. Marie thanked Jeanne for what she sent for the "vide-poche" (a tidy for pocket items), a piece of work "Our Mother" was happy to give her.

- People: Jeanne La Néele (née Guérin); Dr Francis La Néele, her husband; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Je demande aussi que la pharmacie soit enfin vendue » ; « quitte à prendre pour moi s'il le faut les épreuves qu'Il leur réserve »
- Translation: "I also ask that the pharmacy may at last be sold." / "even if I have to take on myself the trials He keeps for them"

### F-OW-128 — The "Céline" flower that bloomed a month early
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that the winter just past had been the harshest ever, so all flowers were late. The "Céline" flower, "the flower of my childhood", bloomed a month early. She read it as an image of suffering making Céline's soul grow. She said her desires for martyrdom were being reborn: "blood for blood and life for life".
- When / where: 20 Oct 1891; fourth feast-day letter from the Carmel
- People: Céline
- Source: LT 132 (to Céline, 20 Oct 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-132-a-celine-20-octobre-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « jamais l'hiver n'avait été si rigoureux que l'année dernière » ; « elle s'est épanouie un mois avant l'époque de sa floraison »
- Translation: "Never had the winter been as harsh as last year." / "It opened a month before its flowering time."
- Other sources: LT 124 (the "Céline" flowers)
- New to English readers: yes ("la fleur Céline": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She reports a natural event read as a sign. It is not a miracle claim.
### F-OW-129 — A feast gift made from her father's hair
- Claim: For Mme Guérin's feast in 1891, the Carmel gave a small present made from Louis Martin's hair. "Our good Mother" had it made. Thérèse was moved to see "these poor hairs" loved by the father God had taken from them. They were given to the one who, after that father, was dearest to her. She also wrote that God had seemed to take from her in childhood a joy she had never known (a mother), and that her aunt had received a mother's tenderness for her.
- When / where: 16 Nov 1891
- People: Mme Guérin; Louis Martin (then in Caen); "notre bonne Mère" (prioress, not named)
- Source: LT 133 (to Mme Guérin, 16 Nov 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-133-a-mme-guerin-16-novembre-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Mon cœur est rempli d'émotion en voyant ces pauvres cheveux qui sans doute n'ont d'autre valeur que le travail délicat et la grâce de leur arrangement, mais qui cependant étaient aimés de celui que le bon Dieu nous a ravi »
- Translation: "My heart is full of emotion seeing these poor hairs. No doubt their only value is the delicate work and the grace of their arrangement, but they were loved by the one God has taken from us."
- Other sources: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the fact of the gift. The letter does not describe the object's form.

### F-OW-130 — Mère Geneviève's death and "the last tear of a saint"
- Claim: Mère Geneviève once told her: "Serve God with peace and joy; remember, my child, our God is the God of peace." Thérèse thought it a revelation; the next Sunday Geneviève said she had had none. At her death (5 Dec 1891, "the first time I was present at a death") Thérèse stood at the foot of the bed for two hours feeling "a kind of insensibility", then at the moment of death felt sudden joy. She saw a tear on the dying nun's eyelid; that evening, unseen, she took it with a small piece of fine linen, and she wears it with her vows. She dreamed Geneviève said three times, "To you I leave my heart."
- When / where: 1890–5 Dec 1891, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse (foundress)
- Source: Ms A 78r–79r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « prendre pour relique la dernière larme d'une Sainte »
- Translation: "to take as a relic the last tear of a saint"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-131 — Influenza epidemic, winter 1891–1892: preparing the dead
- Claim: A month after Mère Geneviève's death influenza struck; Thérèse was one of three sisters still on their feet. "My 19th birthday was celebrated with a death, soon followed by two others." Alone in the sacristy (her senior sister very ill), she prepared funerals. One morning she felt Sr Madeleine was dead; the dormitory was dark; she went in and found her dressed and lying on her straw mattress; "I had not the slightest fear"; she brought a candle and a crown of roses. She was alone with the infirmarian the evening the subprioress died. She received Communion every day, "when the others did not have that happiness".
- When / where: Dec 1891 – Jan 1892, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Madeleine (du Saint-Sacrement); the subprioress (Mère Fébronie, not named here); the infirmarian
- Source: Ms A 79r–79v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Le jour de mes 19 ans fut fêté par une mort, bientôt suivie de deux autres. »
- Translation: "My nineteenth birthday was celebrated with a death, soon followed by two more."
- New to English readers: yes (not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-132 — The double daisy in the Carmel meadow, and a relic of Mère Geneviève
- Claim: For Céline's 23rd birthday, Thérèse sent a double daisy she had found in the Carmel meadow: two stems grown into one. She described how daisies turn to follow the sun all day, and close and turn pink again at night. The flower she sent was "a relic", because it had rested in the hands of "our holy Mother Geneviève" and she had blessed Céline and Thérèse.
- When / where: 26 Apr 1892, the Carmel meadow ("la prairie du Carmel")
- People: Céline; Mère Geneviève (d. Dec 1891, from outside knowledge; the letter calls her "notre Sainte Mère Geneviève")
- Source: LT 134 (to Céline, 26 Apr 1892), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-134-a-celine-26-avril-1892/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « au lieu d'une pâquerette il y en avait bien deux distinctes » ; « la petite fleur que je t'envoie est une relique car elle a reposé entre les mains de notre Sainte Mère Geneviève »
- Translation: "instead of one daisy there were really two separate ones" / "the little flower I am sending you is a relic, because it rested in the hands of our holy Mother Geneviève"
- Other sources: Ms A tells of Mère Geneviève's death
- New to English readers: yes ("double pâquerette": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is the first letter after a five-month gap (Nov 1891 to Apr 1892). The gap covers the influenza epidemic and Mère Geneviève's death, which no letter in this file describes.
### F-OW-133 — Two hosts
- Claim: Once, troubled before Communion, she thought: "If I receive only half a host today, I'll believe Jesus comes reluctantly." For the first time in her life the priest gave her two separate hosts.
- When / where: c. 1892, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: the chaplain (unnamed)
- Source: Ms A 80r
- Source type: own-writing

- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. VIII). See M-OW-06.

### F-OW-134 — "What have we to envy priests?" and a request for heather
- Claim: Thérèse told Céline that the Carmelite apostolate of prayer is "higher than that of the word". Their mission was to form "gospel workers" who would save thousands of souls, "of whom we shall be the mothers… what have we to envy priests?" In a P.S. she said the Rule did not let them send Jeanne a bouquet for her feast. She asked Céline to wish it for them. "If you found some heather, it would please me."
- When / where: 15 Aug 1892. Céline was in the country ("les vastes solitudes", probably La Musse; the place is not named).
- People: Céline; Jeanne La Néele
- Source: LT 135 (to Céline, 15 Aug 1892), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-135-a-celine-15-aout-1892/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Je trouve que notre part est bien belle, qu'avons-nous à envier aux prêtres ?... » ; « la règle ne nous permet pas de le faire » ; « Si tu trouvais de la bruyère, cela me ferait plaisir. »
- Translation: "I find our share very beautiful. What have we to envy priests?…" / "the Rule does not allow us to do it" / "If you found some heather, it would please me."
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre XIIe (15 Aug 1892) keeps the prayer passage. It changes "aux prêtres" to "aux prêtres du Seigneur" and cuts the P.S.
- New to English readers: partial. The main passage is in 1911. The heather, the Rule on bouquets and the exact wording are new.
- Confidence: high
- Notes: none
### F-OW-135 — Marie Guérin, "the little Doctor of the rue de l'Oratoire"
- Claim: Thérèse thanked the Guérins for her feast gifts: a big packet of chocolate, which "delighted our little provisoire" (the sister in charge of provisions), and a coffee cream. She teased Marie Guérin as "the little Doctor of the rue de l'Oratoire", nursing her sick mother and Jeanne, who was convalescent after a relapse. She asked Marie, a "distinguished pastry cook", to make pastries so light that Jeanne could eat them without harm. She apologised for her "ugly handwriting".

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « le gros paquet de chocolat qui a bien réjoui notre petite provisoire » ; « Les bouchées à la reine faites par un pâtissier aussi distingué que toi me semblent un plat bien délicat pour des carmélites »
- Translation: "the big packet of chocolate, which delighted our little provisioner" / "Vol-au-vents made by a pastry cook as distinguished as you seem to me a very refined dish for Carmelites"
- Other sources: LT 146, LT 178 (Marie's gaiety)

### F-OW-136 — Her "simplicities" with the Virgin: "I am happier than you"
- Claim: Thérèse confided to Céline one of her "simplicities" with the Virgin Mary. She sometimes told her: "I find I am happier than you, for I have you as Mother, and you have no Blessed Virgin to love." She added: "No doubt the Blessed Virgin must laugh at my naivety, and yet what I tell her is very true!"

- Translation: "But my good Blessed Virgin, I find I am happier than you, because I have you for Mother, and you have no Blessed Virgin to love…" / "No doubt the Blessed Virgin must laugh at my naivety."

### F-OW-137 — "Come down quickly": the retreat light of 1892
- Claim: During her 1892 retreat, Thérèse understood the words to Zacchaeus, "Make haste and come down". They must "come down" to be so poor that they have nowhere to lay their head, so that Jesus could lodge in them. Her heart was empty of creatures but "not quite empty of me". - People: Céline

- Translation: "I feel that mine is not quite empty of me, and that is why Jesus tells me to come down."

- New to English readers: partial

- Notes: none
### F-OW-138 — Her father "recalled from exile" (1892)
- Claim: In Nov 1892 Thérèse wrote that God had filled her heart with sweet consolation "this year" by "recalling from his exile my dear little Father". - Translation: "This year God filled my heart with a very sweet consolation by recalling my dear little Father from his exile."
- Other sources: LT 146 (father at La Musse, "like Job"); LT 155 (the illness "the greatest cross I could have imagined")
